\end{itemize}

The default classroom path is to use the cached data so the analysis
work is reproducible without internet access.

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[numbers=left,,]
\FunctionTok{library}\NormalTok{(readr)}
\FunctionTok{library}\NormalTok{(dplyr)}

\NormalTok{candidate\_paths }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \FunctionTok{c}\NormalTok{(}
  \StringTok{"examples/nhanes{-}equity/data/nhanes\_equity\_v6.csv"}\NormalTok{,}
  \StringTok{"../../examples/nhanes{-}equity/data/nhanes\_equity\_v6.csv"}
\NormalTok{)}

\NormalTok{nhanes\_path }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}}\NormalTok{ candidate\_paths[}\FunctionTok{file.exists}\NormalTok{(candidate\_paths)][}\DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{]}
\ControlFlowTok{if}\NormalTok{ (}\FunctionTok{is.na}\NormalTok{(nhanes\_path)) \{}
  \FunctionTok{stop}\NormalTok{(}\StringTok{"Could not find examples/nhanes{-}equity/data/nhanes\_equity\_v6.csv"}\NormalTok{)}
\NormalTok{\}}

\NormalTok{nhanes\_analysis }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \FunctionTok{read\_csv}\NormalTok{(nhanes\_path, }\AttributeTok{show\_col\_types =} \ConstantTok{FALSE}\NormalTok{)}

\NormalTok{nhanes\_analysis }\SpecialCharTok{|\textgreater{}}
  \FunctionTok{summarise}\NormalTok{(}
    \AttributeTok{rows =} \FunctionTok{n}\NormalTok{(),}
    \AttributeTok{missing\_bmi =} \FunctionTok{sum}\NormalTok{(}\FunctionTok{is.na}\NormalTok{(BMI)),}
    \AttributeTok{missing\_income =} \FunctionTok{sum}\NormalTok{(}\FunctionTok{is.na}\NormalTok{(IncomeGroup))}
\NormalTok{  )}
\CommentTok{\#\textgreater{} \# A tibble: 1 x 3}
\CommentTok{\#\textgreater{}     rows missing\_bmi missing\_income}
\CommentTok{\#\textgreater{}    \textless{}int\textgreater{}       \textless{}int\textgreater{}          \textless{}int\textgreater{}}
\CommentTok{\#\textgreater{} 1 105626        9356           9415}

\NormalTok{nhanes\_analysis }\SpecialCharTok{|\textgreater{}}
  \FunctionTok{filter}\NormalTok{(}\SpecialCharTok{!}\FunctionTok{is.na}\NormalTok{(BMI), }\SpecialCharTok{!}\FunctionTok{is.na}\NormalTok{(IncomeGroup), Age }\SpecialCharTok{\textgreater{}=} \DecValTok{20}\NormalTok{, Age }\SpecialCharTok{\textless{}=} \DecValTok{80}\NormalTok{) }\SpecialCharTok{|\textgreater{}}
  \FunctionTok{group\_by}\NormalTok{(IncomeGroup) }\SpecialCharTok{|\textgreater{}}
  \FunctionTok{summarise}\NormalTok{(}
    \AttributeTok{n =} \FunctionTok{n}\NormalTok{(),}
    \AttributeTok{mean\_bmi =} \FunctionTok{round}\NormalTok{(}\FunctionTok{mean}\NormalTok{(BMI), }\DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{),}
    \AttributeTok{.groups =} \StringTok{"drop"}
\NormalTok{  )}
\CommentTok{\#\textgreater{} \# A tibble: 3 x 3}
\CommentTok{\#\textgreater{}   IncomeGroup            n mean\_bmi}
\CommentTok{\#\textgreater{}   \textless{}chr\textgreater{}              \textless{}int\textgreater{}    \textless{}dbl\textgreater{}}
\CommentTok{\#\textgreater{} 1 High Income (\textgreater{}3.5) 16330     28.6}
\CommentTok{\#\textgreater{} 2 Low Income (\textless{}1.3)  15293     29.4}
\CommentTok{\#\textgreater{} 3 Middle Income      19228     29.3}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\section*{What students leave with}\label{what-students-leave-with-1}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{What students leave with}

\markright{What students leave with}

One polished descriptive visualization and a reusable checklist for
auditing AI-assisted plots before they appear in a report, dashboard, or
slide.

\chapter*{Why Visualize, and What For}\label{why-visualize-and-what-for}
\addcontentsline{toc}{chapter}{Why Visualize, and What For}

\markboth{Why Visualize, and What For}{Why Visualize, and What For}

\section*{Goal}\label{goal-1}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Goal}

\markright{Goal}

Before you write any \texttt{ggplot()} code, get clear on what a plot is
\emph{for} in a health-data project. A figure is a \textbf{claim about
evidence} --- a reader looks at it, decides what it says, and walks away
with a belief. Your job for the next two weeks is to make sure that
belief matches what the data can actually support.

\section*{The four-step pipeline you will
follow}\label{the-four-step-pipeline-you-will-follow}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{The four-step pipeline you will follow}

\markright{The four-step pipeline you will follow}

This week's pages all support the same short pipeline. Every
visualization you make for the course should run through it:

\begin{enumerate}
\def\labelenumi{\arabic{enumi}.}
\tightlist
\item
  \textbf{Load and inspect} the data so you know what is actually there.
\item
  \textbf{Plot} a clear descriptive summary --- one figure, one
  comparison.
\item
  \textbf{Audit} the figure against the data and the question.
\item
  \textbf{Caption} the figure so a reader can interpret it without
  reading your code.
\end{enumerate}

If any step is skipped, the figure is fragile. If all four are present,
the figure can survive an AI review, a peer review, or a project grader.

\section*{What a ``good'' descriptive plot
does}\label{what-a-good-descriptive-plot-does}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{What a ``good'' descriptive plot does}

\markright{What a ``good'' descriptive plot does}

A descriptive plot in this course does three things:

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  It names the \textbf{statistic} (a count, mean, proportion, median).
\item
  It names the \textbf{comparison} (across what groups, time, or
  condition).
\item
  It names the \textbf{limitation} (what the figure does \emph{not}
  support --- e.g., not a causal claim, not a national estimate).
\end{itemize}

A ``bad'' plot quietly drops one of these and lets the reader fill in
the gap. Your audit habit is what catches that.

\section*{Where AI fits}\label{where-ai-fits}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Where AI fits}

\markright{Where AI fits}

You will use an AI co-pilot to draft \texttt{ggplot2} code. You will not
let it choose your scale, your title, or your caption without review.
The Week 3 rule still holds: every AI-drafted chunk gets a
\texttt{\#\ AI\ prompt:\ ...\ \#\ Verified:\ ...} comment line, and you
check the column names, the missingness handling, and the claim the
figure makes.

\section*{What you produce this week}\label{what-you-produce-this-week}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{What you produce this week}

\markright{What you produce this week}

Two artifacts:

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  One revised, captioned \texttt{ggplot2} figure based on the NHANES
  Health Equity CSV (the Assignment 5 deliverable).
\item
  A short M1 visual plan paragraph naming the figure direction for your
  term project (one part of the Milestone 1 proposal due the same week).
\end{itemize}

\begin{tcolorbox}[enhanced jigsaw, colback=white, opacitybacktitle=0.6, breakable, coltitle=black, bottomtitle=1mm, bottomrule=.15mm, left=2mm, opacityback=0, leftrule=.75mm, rightrule=.15mm, toptitle=1mm, colbacktitle=quarto-callout-tip-color!10!white, titlerule=0mm, colframe=quarto-callout-tip-color-frame, arc=.35mm, title=\textcolor{quarto-callout-tip-color}{\faLightbulb}\hspace{0.5em}{Tip}, toprule=.15mm]

The plot you make for A5 does not need to be the same as the figure you
sketch for M1. A5 is practice on a known dataset; M1 is your team's plan
for your own project. The audit standard is the same in both places.

\end{tcolorbox}

\chapter*{Grammar of a ggplot, and Your First
Plot}\label{grammar-of-a-ggplot-and-your-first-plot}
\addcontentsline{toc}{chapter}{Grammar of a ggplot, and Your First Plot}

\markboth{Grammar of a ggplot, and Your First Plot}{Grammar of a ggplot,
and Your First Plot}

\section*{Where this fits}\label{where-this-fits}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Where this fits}

\markright{Where this fits}

\hyperref[why-visualize-and-what-for]{viz01} framed visualization as a
claim about evidence. This page teaches the five moving parts of any
\texttt{ggplot2} figure and walks you through your first plot on the
NHANES Health Equity CSV. Everything builds from these five parts.

\section*{The five moving parts}\label{the-five-moving-parts}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{The five moving parts}

\markright{The five moving parts}

Every ggplot is built from the same five ingredients. You will see all
five in the worked example on the next page, so the names matter.

\begin{longtable}[]{@{}
  >{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{(\linewidth - 4\tabcolsep) * \real{0.3333}}
  >{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{(\linewidth - 4\tabcolsep) * \real{0.3333}}
  >{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{(\linewidth - 4\tabcolsep) * \real{0.3333}}@{}}
\toprule\noalign{}
\begin{minipage}[b]{\linewidth}\raggedright
Part
\end{minipage} & \begin{minipage}[b]{\linewidth}\raggedright
What it answers
\end{minipage} & \begin{minipage}[b]{\linewidth}\raggedright
A beginner-friendly analogy
\end{minipage} \\
\midrule\noalign{}
\endhead
\bottomrule\noalign{}
\endlastfoot
\textbf{Data} & What table are we plotting? & The contents of one box \\
\textbf{Mapping (\texttt{aes})} & Which variable goes where (x, y,
color, group)? & Telling R which column to draw on which axis \\
\textbf{Geometry (\texttt{geom\_*})} & What kind of mark (point, line,
bar)? & The shape of the ink on the page \\
\textbf{Scale and coordinates} & How are values stretched along an axis?
& The ruler under the ink \\
\textbf{Labels and theme} & What does the reader see in plain text? &
The caption around the picture \\
\end{longtable}

If a reader cannot answer ``which statistic? across what groups? with
what limitation?'' from your figure, one of these five parts is doing
too little.

\section*{Setup}\label{setup}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Setup}

\markright{Setup}

Open your Codespace and start a new code chunk in a \texttt{.qmd} file.
Run:

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[numbers=left,,]
\FunctionTok{library}\NormalTok{(readr)}
\FunctionTok{library}\NormalTok{(dplyr)}
\FunctionTok{library}\NormalTok{(ggplot2)}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\begin{tcolorbox}[enhanced jigsaw, colback=white, opacitybacktitle=0.6, breakable, coltitle=black, bottomtitle=1mm, bottomrule=.15mm, left=2mm, opacityback=0, leftrule=.75mm, rightrule=.15mm, toptitle=1mm, colbacktitle=quarto-callout-warning-color!10!white, titlerule=0mm, colframe=quarto-callout-warning-color-frame, arc=.35mm, title=\textcolor{quarto-callout-warning-color}{\faExclamationTriangle}\hspace{0.5em}{Warning}, toprule=.15mm]

\texttt{library(ggplot2)} is required every session. If you see ``could
not find function \texttt{ggplot}'', run the \texttt{library()} line
first.

\end{tcolorbox}

\section*{Load the cached NHANES CSV}\label{load-the-cached-nhanes-csv}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Load the cached NHANES CSV}

\markright{Load the cached NHANES CSV}

This is the same file you will use for the worked example and your A5
deliverable. The candidate-path pattern below works whether your
\texttt{.qmd} is at the repository root or under
\texttt{weeks/week06-visualization/}.

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[numbers=left,,]
\NormalTok{candidate\_paths }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \FunctionTok{c}\NormalTok{(}
  \StringTok{"examples/nhanes{-}equity/data/nhanes\_equity\_v6.csv"}\NormalTok{,}
  \StringTok{"../../examples/nhanes{-}equity/data/nhanes\_equity\_v6.csv"}
\NormalTok{)}
\NormalTok{data\_path }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}}\NormalTok{ candidate\_paths[}\FunctionTok{file.exists}\NormalTok{(candidate\_paths)][}\DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{]}
\NormalTok{nhanes }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \FunctionTok{read\_csv}\NormalTok{(data\_path, }\AttributeTok{show\_col\_types =} \ConstantTok{FALSE}\NormalTok{)}

\FunctionTok{glimpse}\NormalTok{(nhanes)}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\texttt{glimpse()} shows the column names and types. Read them
carefully. Misspelling a column name (e.g., \texttt{bmi} when the data
has \texttt{BMI}) is the \#1 source of broken ggplots in this course.

\begin{tcolorbox}[enhanced jigsaw, colback=white, opacitybacktitle=0.6, breakable, coltitle=black, bottomtitle=1mm, bottomrule=.15mm, left=2mm, opacityback=0, leftrule=.75mm, rightrule=.15mm, toptitle=1mm, colbacktitle=quarto-callout-note-color!10!white, titlerule=0mm, colframe=quarto-callout-note-color-frame, arc=.35mm, title=\textcolor{quarto-callout-note-color}{\faInfo}\hspace{0.5em}{Tester note}, toprule=.15mm]

Capture: a screenshot of the Codespaces Quarto preview after
\texttt{glimpse(nhanes)} runs, showing the column names. New students
rely on this to spot case-sensitive column names like \texttt{BMI} and
\texttt{IncomeGroup}.

\end{tcolorbox}

\section*{Your first plot}\label{your-first-plot}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Your first plot}

\markright{Your first plot}

Here is the smallest meaningful ggplot you can build from this dataset:
a scatter of one numeric variable against age, with no grouping and no
transformation. Try it as is, then change \texttt{Weight} to
\texttt{BMI} or \texttt{Height} and re-render.

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[numbers=left,,]
\FunctionTok{ggplot}\NormalTok{(nhanes, }\FunctionTok{aes}\NormalTok{(}\AttributeTok{x =}\NormalTok{ Age, }\AttributeTok{y =}\NormalTok{ Weight)) }\SpecialCharTok{+}
  \FunctionTok{geom\_point}\NormalTok{(}\AttributeTok{alpha =} \FloatTok{0.2}\NormalTok{) }\SpecialCharTok{+}
  \FunctionTok{labs}\NormalTok{(}
    \AttributeTok{title =} \StringTok{"Weight by age, NHANES Health Equity classroom dataset"}\NormalTok{,}
    \AttributeTok{subtitle =} \StringTok{"One point per record; unweighted, descriptive only"}\NormalTok{,}
    \AttributeTok{x =} \StringTok{"Age (years)"}\NormalTok{,}
    \AttributeTok{y =} \StringTok{"Weight (kg)"}
\NormalTok{  ) }\SpecialCharTok{+}
  \FunctionTok{theme\_minimal}\NormalTok{()}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

Map the five parts onto this code so the grammar feels concrete:

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  \textbf{Data:} \texttt{nhanes}
\item
  \textbf{Mapping:} \texttt{aes(x\ =\ Age,\ y\ =\ Weight)}
\item
  \textbf{Geometry:} \texttt{geom\_point(alpha\ =\ 0.2)} (translucent
  points so overlap is visible)
\item
  \textbf{Scale and coordinates:} the default linear axes (you have not
  changed them)
\item
  \textbf{Labels and theme:} \texttt{labs(...)\ +\ theme\_minimal()}
\end{itemize}

\begin{tcolorbox}[enhanced jigsaw, colback=white, opacitybacktitle=0.6, breakable, coltitle=black, bottomtitle=1mm, bottomrule=.15mm, left=2mm, opacityback=0, leftrule=.75mm, rightrule=.15mm, toptitle=1mm, colbacktitle=quarto-callout-note-color!10!white, titlerule=0mm, colframe=quarto-callout-note-color-frame, arc=.35mm, title=\textcolor{quarto-callout-note-color}{\faInfo}\hspace{0.5em}{Tester note}, toprule=.15mm]

Capture: a screenshot of the rendered scatter plot. Confirm the axis
labels read ``Age (years)'' and ``Weight (kg)'' so students can compare
against their own first attempt.

\end{tcolorbox}

\section*{Three things to check on every
plot}\label{three-things-to-check-on-every-plot}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Three things to check on every plot}

\markright{Three things to check on every plot}

After you run any ggplot, ask:

\begin{enumerate}
\def\labelenumi{\arabic{enumi}.}
\tightlist
\item
  \textbf{Column names match the data.} Did \texttt{glimpse()} confirm
  \texttt{BMI} is uppercase, not \texttt{bmi}?
\item
  \textbf{Axes tell a clear story.} Are the units readable? Is the range
  honest?
\item
  \textbf{Title and subtitle match what is plotted.} Does the title
  overstate (``BMI rises with age across Canada'')? If yes, soften it
  (``Weight by age in the prepared classroom dataset'').
\end{enumerate}

\markright{Where this goes next}

\chapter*{Worked Example: Descriptive BMI
Visualization}\label{worked-example-descriptive-bmi-visualization}
\addcontentsline{toc}{chapter}{Worked Example: Descriptive BMI
Visualization}

\markboth{Worked Example: Descriptive BMI Visualization}{Worked Example:
Descriptive BMI Visualization}

\section*{Where this fits}\label{where-this-fits-1}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Where this fits}

\markright{Where this fits}

You learned the grammar of a ggplot and made one minimal scatter on the
\hyperref[grammar-of-a-ggplot-and-your-first-plot]{previous page}. This
page is the first end-to-end visualization of the term: load the cached
NHANES Health Equity CSV, prepare a small descriptive summary, render
one labeled figure, and write a caption that does not overclaim. We will
return to this same figure in \href{viz04-misleading-design.qmd}{viz04}
to see how easy it is to spoil with a few ``improvements.''

\section*{Goal}\label{goal-2}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Goal}

\markright{Goal}

This worked example uses the NHANES Health Equity CSV snapshot to build
one descriptive visualization. The plot summarizes mean BMI by income
group across NHANES cycle. It is \textbf{unweighted} and should be
interpreted as a classroom visualization exercise, not a population
estimate.

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[numbers=left,,]
\FunctionTok{library}\NormalTok{(readr)}
\FunctionTok{library}\NormalTok{(dplyr)}
\FunctionTok{library}\NormalTok{(ggplot2)}

\NormalTok{candidate\_paths }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \FunctionTok{c}\NormalTok{(}
  \StringTok{"examples/nhanes{-}equity/data/nhanes\_equity\_v6.csv"}\NormalTok{,}
  \StringTok{"../../examples/nhanes{-}equity/data/nhanes\_equity\_v6.csv"}
\NormalTok{)}

\NormalTok{data\_path }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}}\NormalTok{ candidate\_paths[}\FunctionTok{file.exists}\NormalTok{(candidate\_paths)][}\DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{]}
\ControlFlowTok{if}\NormalTok{ (}\FunctionTok{is.na}\NormalTok{(data\_path)) \{}
  \FunctionTok{stop}\NormalTok{(}\StringTok{"Could not find examples/nhanes{-}equity/data/nhanes\_equity\_v6.csv"}\NormalTok{)}
\NormalTok{\}}

\NormalTok{nhanes }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \FunctionTok{read\_csv}\NormalTok{(data\_path, }\AttributeTok{show\_col\_types =} \ConstantTok{FALSE}\NormalTok{)}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\section*{Prepare a Descriptive
Summary}\label{prepare-a-descriptive-summary}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Prepare a Descriptive Summary}

\markright{Prepare a Descriptive Summary}

Keep the summary intentionally simple. We are describing the prepared
class dataset, not estimating a national trend.

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[numbers=left,,]
\NormalTok{bmi\_by\_income\_cycle }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}}\NormalTok{ nhanes }\SpecialCharTok{|\textgreater{}}
  \FunctionTok{filter}\NormalTok{(}
    \SpecialCharTok{!}\FunctionTok{is.na}\NormalTok{(BMI),}
    \SpecialCharTok{!}\FunctionTok{is.na}\NormalTok{(IncomeGroup),}
    \SpecialCharTok{!}\FunctionTok{is.na}\NormalTok{(Cycle),}
\NormalTok{    Age }\SpecialCharTok{\textgreater{}=} \DecValTok{20}\NormalTok{,}
\NormalTok{    Age }\SpecialCharTok{\textless{}=} \DecValTok{80}
\NormalTok{  ) }\SpecialCharTok{|\textgreater{}}
  \FunctionTok{group\_by}\NormalTok{(Cycle, IncomeGroup) }\SpecialCharTok{|\textgreater{}}
  \FunctionTok{summarise}\NormalTok{(}
    \AttributeTok{n =} \FunctionTok{n}\NormalTok{(),}
    \AttributeTok{mean\_bmi =} \FunctionTok{mean}\NormalTok{(BMI),}
    \AttributeTok{.groups =} \StringTok{"drop"}
\NormalTok{  ) }\SpecialCharTok{|\textgreater{}}
  \FunctionTok{filter}\NormalTok{(n }\SpecialCharTok{\textgreater{}=} \DecValTok{30}\NormalTok{)}

\NormalTok{bmi\_by\_income\_cycle}
\CommentTok{\#\textgreater{} \# A tibble: 33 x 4}
\CommentTok{\#\textgreater{}    Cycle     IncomeGroup            n mean\_bmi}
\CommentTok{\#\textgreater{}    \textless{}chr\textgreater{}     \textless{}chr\textgreater{}              \textless{}int\textgreater{}    \textless{}dbl\textgreater{}}
\CommentTok{\#\textgreater{}  1 1999{-}2000 High Income (\textgreater{}3.5)  1123     27.9}
\CommentTok{\#\textgreater{}  2 1999{-}2000 Low Income (\textless{}1.3)   1091     28.8}
\CommentTok{\#\textgreater{}  3 1999{-}2000 Middle Income       1327     29.0}
\CommentTok{\#\textgreater{}  4 2001{-}2002 High Income (\textgreater{}3.5)  1491     28.1}
\CommentTok{\#\textgreater{}  5 2001{-}2002 Low Income (\textless{}1.3)   1089     28.7}
\CommentTok{\#\textgreater{}  6 2001{-}2002 Middle Income       1561     28.3}
\CommentTok{\#\textgreater{}  7 2003{-}2004 High Income (\textgreater{}3.5)  1284     28.4}
\CommentTok{\#\textgreater{}  8 2003{-}2004 Low Income (\textless{}1.3)   1183     28.6}
\CommentTok{\#\textgreater{}  9 2003{-}2004 Middle Income       1573     28.8}
\CommentTok{\#\textgreater{} 10 2005{-}2006 High Income (\textgreater{}3.5)  1480     28.5}
\CommentTok{\#\textgreater{} \# i 23 more rows}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\section*{Plot}\label{plot}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Plot}

\markright{Plot}

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[numbers=left,,]
\NormalTok{bmi\_plot }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \FunctionTok{ggplot}\NormalTok{(}
\NormalTok{  bmi\_by\_income\_cycle,}
  \FunctionTok{aes}\NormalTok{(}\AttributeTok{x =}\NormalTok{ Cycle, }\AttributeTok{y =}\NormalTok{ mean\_bmi, }\AttributeTok{color =}\NormalTok{ IncomeGroup, }\AttributeTok{group =}\NormalTok{ IncomeGroup)}
\NormalTok{) }\SpecialCharTok{+}
  \FunctionTok{geom\_line}\NormalTok{(}\AttributeTok{linewidth =} \FloatTok{0.8}\NormalTok{) }\SpecialCharTok{+}
  \FunctionTok{geom\_point}\NormalTok{(}\AttributeTok{size =} \DecValTok{2}\NormalTok{) }\SpecialCharTok{+}
  \FunctionTok{labs}\NormalTok{(}
    \AttributeTok{title =} \StringTok{"Mean BMI by income group across NHANES cycle"}\NormalTok{,}
    \AttributeTok{subtitle =} \StringTok{"Adults age 20{-}80 in the prepared class dataset; unweighted descriptive summary"}\NormalTok{,}
    \AttributeTok{x =} \StringTok{"NHANES cycle"}\NormalTok{,}
    \AttributeTok{y =} \StringTok{"Mean BMI"}\NormalTok{,}
    \AttributeTok{color =} \StringTok{"Income group"}\NormalTok{,}
    \AttributeTok{caption =} \StringTok{"Caption template: Mean BMI is summarized descriptively from the cached class dataset. Values are unweighted, exclude missing BMI/income/cycle values, and should not be interpreted as causal effects or population estimates."}
\NormalTok{  ) }\SpecialCharTok{+}
  \FunctionTok{theme\_minimal}\NormalTok{(}\AttributeTok{base\_size =} \DecValTok{12}\NormalTok{) }\SpecialCharTok{+}
  \FunctionTok{theme}\NormalTok{(}
    \AttributeTok{axis.text.x =} \FunctionTok{element\_text}\NormalTok{(}\AttributeTok{angle =} \DecValTok{45}\NormalTok{, }\AttributeTok{hjust =} \DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{),}
    \AttributeTok{legend.position =} \StringTok{"bottom"}
\NormalTok{  )}

\NormalTok{bmi\_plot}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\pandocbounded{\includegraphics[keepaspectratio]{weeks/week06-visualization/viz03-worked-example_files/figure-pdf/plot-bmi-by-income-cycle-1.pdf}}

\section*{Optional Plotly Check}\label{optional-plotly-check}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Optional Plotly Check}

\markright{Optional Plotly Check}

The core assignment uses the static \texttt{ggplot2} figure above. If
\texttt{plotly} is installed, you can preview the same plot
interactively while checking labels and tooltips. This extension is
optional because static Quarto output is the supported submission path.

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[numbers=left,,]
\CommentTok{\# plotly::ggplotly(bmi\_plot)}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\section*{Caption Template}\label{caption-template}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Caption Template}

\markright{Caption Template}

Use this structure when revising your own plot:

\begin{quote}
This figure shows {[}measure{]} by {[}group{]} across
{[}time/category{]} using the prepared NHANES Health Equity classroom
dataset. The summary is descriptive and unweighted. Records with missing
{[}variables{]} were excluded. The plot supports comparison of patterns,
not causal claims or population-level estimates.
\end{quote}

\section*{Mini Visual Audit
Checklist}\label{mini-visual-audit-checklist}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Mini Visual Audit Checklist}

\markright{Mini Visual Audit Checklist}

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  Does the title describe what is plotted without overstating the claim?
\item
  Is it clear whether the statistic is weighted or unweighted?
\item
  Are axes, groups, and units labeled plainly?
\item
  Are missing values or exclusions disclosed?
\item
  Is the scale chosen to support honest comparison rather than
  exaggeration?
\item
  Does the caption state the limitation that this is descriptive, not
  causal?
\end{itemize}

\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Where this goes next}

\markright{Where this goes next}

Hold onto this figure. The \href{viz04-misleading-design.qmd}{next page}
walks through six common ways a plot like this can mislead a reader, and

\chapter*{How a Plot Can Mislead}\label{how-a-plot-can-mislead}
\addcontentsline{toc}{chapter}{How a Plot Can Mislead}

\markboth{How a Plot Can Mislead}{How a Plot Can Mislead}

\section*{Where this fits}\label{where-this-fits-2}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Where this fits}

\markright{Where this fits}

You have a clean, captioned worked example
(\hyperref[worked-example-descriptive-bmi-visualization]{viz03}). This
page is the audit lens: six common ways a figure that \emph{looks fine}
can quietly mislead a reader. Each one shows up in real research, and
each one is something an AI co-pilot will produce if you do not push
back.

\section*{Six failure modes}\label{six-failure-modes}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Six failure modes}

\markright{Six failure modes}

For each failure mode, you will see the problem in one sentence and the
fix in one sentence. Studio time in
\href{viz06-studio-visual-audit.qmd}{viz06} revisits these on a
deliberately flawed plot.

\subsection*{1. Truncated y-axis
exaggeration}\label{truncated-y-axis-exaggeration}
\addcontentsline{toc}{subsection}{1. Truncated y-axis exaggeration}

A small difference in means looks huge when the y-axis starts at 27
instead of 0.

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  \textbf{Symptom:} \texttt{coord\_cartesian(ylim\ =\ c(27.5,\ 31.5))}
  on a BMI mean plot, no narrative explanation.
\item
  \textbf{Fix:} Start the axis at 0, or at a value justified in the
  caption. Use \texttt{expand\_limits(y\ =\ 0)} when in doubt.
\end{itemize}

\subsection*{2. Causal title on a descriptive
plot}\label{causal-title-on-a-descriptive-plot}
\addcontentsline{toc}{subsection}{2. Causal title on a descriptive plot}

A title that says ``X causes Y'' is a causal claim. Descriptive
summaries cannot support it.

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  \textbf{Symptom:} ``Income level causes BMI to rise across NHANES
  cycles.''
\item
  \textbf{Fix:} ``Mean BMI by income group across NHANES cycles in the
  prepared classroom dataset.''
\end{itemize}

\subsection*{3. Smoothing across categorical
time}\label{smoothing-across-categorical-time}
\addcontentsline{toc}{subsection}{3. Smoothing across categorical time}

\texttt{geom\_smooth(method\ =\ "lm")} draws a line that \emph{implies}
a continuous linear trend. NHANES cycles are categorical labels, not a
continuous axis.

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  \textbf{Symptom:} A linear regression line layered across cycle bins.
\item
  \textbf{Fix:} Use \texttt{geom\_line()} plus \texttt{geom\_point()} to
  connect descriptive cycle summaries, with no regression overlay.
\end{itemize}

\subsection*{4. Weighted-vs-unweighted
ambiguity}\label{weighted-vs-unweighted-ambiguity}
\addcontentsline{toc}{subsection}{4. Weighted-vs-unweighted ambiguity}

If the subtitle says ``weighted'' but the code does not use a survey
weight variable, the reader has been misled.

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  \textbf{Symptom:} Subtitle: ``Weighted BMI trend.'' Code:
  \texttt{mean(BMI,\ na.rm\ =\ TRUE)} with no weight.
\item
  \textbf{Fix:} State plainly that the summary is unweighted, or compute
  a weighted estimate using the documented weight variable.
\end{itemize}

\subsection*{5. Silent missingness}\label{silent-missingness}
\addcontentsline{toc}{subsection}{5. Silent missingness}

Rows with missing BMI or income disappear without a caption.

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  \textbf{Symptom:} \texttt{filter(!is.na(BMI),\ !is.na(IncomeGroup))}
  deep inside a pipe with no note in the caption.
\item
  \textbf{Fix:} Disclose missing-value exclusions in the caption.
  Better: report missingness counts before the figure (you will do this
  on \href{../week07-eda-ai-audit/eda03-worked-example.qmd}{eda03} next
  week).
\end{itemize}

\subsection*{6. Hidden small-cell
counts}\label{hidden-small-cell-counts}
\addcontentsline{toc}{subsection}{6. Hidden small-cell counts}

A group with \texttt{n\ =\ 7} plots the same way as a group with
\texttt{n\ =\ 700}, but the reader cannot tell them apart.

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  \textbf{Symptom:} A small income group plotted as a line with no
  indication of sparse cells.
\item
  \textbf{Fix:} Filter out groups below a minimum count, \emph{or} label
  them visibly with \texttt{n} annotations, \emph{or} report the counts
  beside the figure.
\end{itemize}

\section*{Why six and not sixty}\label{why-six-and-not-sixty}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Why six and not sixty}

\markright{Why six and not sixty}

You will see other failure modes in the wild --- duplicated colors,
default rainbow palettes, dual axes, missing legends. The six above
account for most of the issues you will catch when auditing an
AI-drafted plot for descriptive health data work in this course. Master
these first.

\section*{A quick audit habit}\label{a-quick-audit-habit}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{A quick audit habit}

\markright{A quick audit habit}

When you finish a plot, ask three questions out loud:

\begin{enumerate}
\def\labelenumi{\arabic{enumi}.}
\tightlist
\item
  Could a reader make a causal claim from this title or caption?
\item
  Could a small difference look bigger than it is because of the axis?
\item
  Is missingness, exclusion, or small-cell count visible to the reader?
\end{enumerate}

If any answer is ``yes'' or ``I don't know,'' revise the figure or the
caption before moving on.

\section*{Where this goes next}\label{where-this-goes-next-3}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Where this goes next}

\markright{Where this goes next}

\href{viz05-ai-prompts-and-audit.qmd}{viz05} shows how to write AI
prompts that produce plots you can defend, and how to audit what the AI
gives you.

\chapter*{AI Prompts You Can Defend}\label{ai-prompts-you-can-defend}
\addcontentsline{toc}{chapter}{AI Prompts You Can Defend}

\markboth{AI Prompts You Can Defend}{AI Prompts You Can Defend}

\section*{Where this fits}\label{where-this-fits-3}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Where this fits}

\markright{Where this fits}

You can build a ggplot from scratch
(\hyperref[grammar-of-a-ggplot-and-your-first-plot]{viz02}) and you can
spot six common failure modes
(\hyperref[how-a-plot-can-mislead]{viz04}). This page connects the two:
how to ask an AI co-pilot for plot code without inheriting its bad
defaults, and how to audit what it gives you.

\section*{Why this matters}\label{why-this-matters-1}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Why this matters}

\markright{Why this matters}

If you paste ``make me a nice plot of NHANES'' into a chat window, the
model will produce something --- possibly with a flashy palette, a trend
line, and a confident title. None of those choices were yours. None of
them were audited. By the time the figure is in your report, you own
them anyway.

The fix is to write prompts that \textbf{constrain the model} instead of
decorate the request.

\section*{Three prompt templates that
work}\label{three-prompt-templates-that-work}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Three prompt templates that work}

\markright{Three prompt templates that work}

\subsection*{Template 1 --- Start from the data, not the
picture}\label{template-1-start-from-the-data-not-the-picture}
\addcontentsline{toc}{subsection}{Template 1 --- Start from the data,
not the picture}

\begin{quote}
I have a data frame called \texttt{bmi\_by\_income\_cycle} with columns
\texttt{Cycle}, \texttt{IncomeGroup}, \texttt{n}, and
\texttt{mean\_bmi}. Write a \texttt{ggplot2} figure that shows
\texttt{mean\_bmi} on the y-axis and \texttt{Cycle} on the x-axis, with
one line per \texttt{IncomeGroup}. The summary is unweighted and
descriptive. Do not add a trend line or smoothing.
\end{quote}

What this prompt does well: it names the data, the variables, the
geometry, and what \emph{not} to add.

\subsection*{Template 2 --- Ask for an honest scale and
labels}\label{template-2-ask-for-an-honest-scale-and-labels}
\addcontentsline{toc}{subsection}{Template 2 --- Ask for an honest scale
and labels}

\begin{quote}
Add \texttt{labs(...)} to the figure above so the title describes the
comparison without making a causal claim, the subtitle states that the
summary is unweighted and uses adults age 20-80, and the caption
discloses that records with missing BMI, income, or cycle were excluded.
\end{quote}

What this prompt does well: it tells the model what each label should
\emph{do}, not just that there should be labels.

\subsection*{Template 3 --- Ask for a caption that names the
limitation}\label{template-3-ask-for-a-caption-that-names-the-limitation}
\addcontentsline{toc}{subsection}{Template 3 --- Ask for a caption that
names the limitation}

\begin{quote}
Write a one-sentence caption for this figure that names the statistic
(mean BMI), the comparison (across NHANES cycles, by income group), the
data source (prepared NHANES Health Equity classroom CSV), the
exclusions (missing values, ages outside 20-80), and one limitation
(descriptive only, not a population estimate).
\end{quote}

What this prompt does well: the model now has a checklist of what the
caption must contain.

\section*{Auditing what the model gives
you}\label{auditing-what-the-model-gives-you}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Auditing what the model gives you}

\markright{Auditing what the model gives you}

After you paste in any AI-drafted plot code, walk through this checklist
before you accept it:

\begin{enumerate}
\def\labelenumi{\arabic{enumi}.}
\tightlist
\item
  \textbf{Column names match the data.} Run \texttt{glimpse(your\_data)}
  and compare.
\item
  \textbf{Geometry is appropriate.} No smoothing across categorical
  cycles. No regression lines on a descriptive summary.
\item
  \textbf{Scale is honest.} Does the y-axis start at 0, or is there a
  stated reason it does not?
\item
  \textbf{Title and subtitle are descriptive.} No ``X causes Y''
  wording.
\item
  \textbf{Caption discloses exclusions and limitation.} Missing values,
  cohort restrictions, weighted vs unweighted.
\item
  \textbf{\texttt{na.rm\ =\ TRUE} is present} wherever the summary uses
  a numeric column that may have \texttt{NA}.
\end{enumerate}

If any item fails, edit the code or the labels before rendering.

\section*{The paper-trail rule (still in
force)}\label{the-paper-trail-rule-still-in-force}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{The paper-trail rule (still in force)}

\markright{The paper-trail rule (still in force)}

From Week 3: every AI-drafted chunk in your \texttt{.qmd} gets a
two-line comment.

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[numbers=left,,]
\CommentTok{\# AI prompt: "Plot mean\_bmi by Cycle, one line per IncomeGroup, no smoothing"}
\CommentTok{\# Verified: columns match glimpse(); no smoother; descriptive title; caption lists exclusions}
\NormalTok{bmi\_plot }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \FunctionTok{ggplot}\NormalTok{(bmi\_by\_income\_cycle, }\FunctionTok{aes}\NormalTok{(...)) }\SpecialCharTok{+}\NormalTok{ ...}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

This is the same audit log you used in Week 3 for data wrangling. The
course AI policy requires it for graded work.

\section*{A note on what NOT to ask the
AI}\label{a-note-on-what-not-to-ask-the-ai}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{A note on what NOT to ask the AI}

\markright{A note on what NOT to ask the AI}

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  Do not paste row-level NHANES data into an external chat window.
  Aggregate first.
\item
  Do not ask the AI to ``decide'' whether income causes BMI. The figure
  is descriptive; causal language is your responsibility to remove.
\item
  Do not accept a ``looks fine to me'' from the model as audit evidence.
  The audit is yours.
\end{itemize}

you apply all of this to a deliberately flawed plot.

\chapter*{In-Class Studio: AI Visual
Audit}\label{in-class-studio-ai-visual-audit}
\addcontentsline{toc}{chapter}{In-Class Studio: AI Visual Audit}

\markboth{In-Class Studio: AI Visual Audit}{In-Class Studio: AI Visual
Audit}

\section*{Where this fits}\label{where-this-fits-4}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Where this fits}

\markright{Where this fits}

You have the worked example
(\hyperref[worked-example-descriptive-bmi-visualization]{viz03}), the
audit categories (\hyperref[how-a-plot-can-mislead]{viz04}), and the
AI-prompt pattern (\hyperref[ai-prompts-you-can-defend]{viz05}). This
page is the in-class studio where you put all three to work: read a
deliberately flawed plot, identify at least three problems, revise the
plot, and write a short audit note. The same workflow is what Assignment
5 expects, so think of this as the dress rehearsal.

\section*{Before you start}\label{before-you-start}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Before you start}

\markright{Before you start}

Take 2 minutes to check that your Codespace is ready:

\begin{enumerate}
\def\labelenumi{\arabic{enumi}.}
\tightlist
\item
  \texttt{library(readr);\ library(dplyr);\ library(ggplot2)} runs
  without error.
\item
  The cached data file resolves from the repository root:
  \texttt{examples/nhanes-equity/data/nhanes\_equity\_v6.csv}.
\item
  Your \texttt{viz03-worked-example.qmd} renders cleanly.
\end{enumerate}

If any of those three fail, raise it with your debugging partner before
moving on.

\section*{Goal}\label{goal-3}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Goal}

\markright{Goal}

Evaluate one AI-generated visualization for correctness, clarity, bias,
aggregation choices, and misleading design. Then revise it so the visual
matches the evidence.

\section*{Inputs}\label{inputs}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Inputs}

\markright{Inputs}

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  Worked example to compare against:
  \texttt{weeks/week06-visualization/viz03-worked-example.qmd}
\item
  Dataset: \texttt{examples/nhanes-equity/data/nhanes\_equity\_v6.csv}
\item
  Flawed plot code (the artifact you will audit):
  \texttt{weeks/week06-visualization/instructor/flawed-plot-code.R}
\end{itemize}

\begin{tcolorbox}[enhanced jigsaw, colback=white, opacitybacktitle=0.6, breakable, coltitle=black, bottomtitle=1mm, bottomrule=.15mm, left=2mm, opacityback=0, leftrule=.75mm, rightrule=.15mm, toptitle=1mm, colbacktitle=quarto-callout-note-color!10!white, titlerule=0mm, colframe=quarto-callout-note-color-frame, arc=.35mm, title=\textcolor{quarto-callout-note-color}{\faInfo}\hspace{0.5em}{Tester note}, toprule=.15mm]

Capture: a screenshot of the rendered flawed plot in the Codespaces
Quarto preview, with the misleading title clearly visible. This will be
referenced by other students during the studio.

\end{tcolorbox}

\section*{Activity Steps}\label{activity-steps}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Activity Steps}

\markright{Activity Steps}

\begin{enumerate}
\def\labelenumi{\arabic{enumi}.}
\tightlist
\item
  Review the worked example and note how it labels the data source,
  statistic, and limitation.
\item
  Open \texttt{instructor/flawed-plot-code.R} and read the comments and
  output labels before you run it.
\item
  Run the script and inspect the rendered figure.
\item
  List at least three issues that could mislead a reader. Tag each one
  with one of the audit categories below.
\item
  Revise the plot so the title, scale, grouping, and caption match the
  evidence. Keep the cohort restriction explicit.
\item
  Write a short note explaining what you changed and why.
\end{enumerate}

\section*{Audit Categories}\label{audit-categories}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Audit Categories}

\markright{Audit Categories}

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  \textbf{Correctness:} Does the plot show what the title and labels
  claim?
\item
  \textbf{Design:} Do scale, color, ordering, and geometry support fair
  comparison?
\item
  \textbf{Interpretation:} Does the caption avoid causal or
  population-level claims that the plot cannot support?
\item
  \textbf{Bias and aggregation:} Could grouping, missingness, or small
  counts hide important variation?
\item
  \textbf{Reproducibility:} Can another person rerun the code from the
  repository root using relative paths?
\end{itemize}

\section*{Required Outputs (studio
draft)}\label{required-outputs-studio-draft}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Required Outputs (studio draft)}

\markright{Required Outputs (studio draft)}

These are the draft files you produce in class. The Assignment 5
deliverable list (\href{viz07-assignment-a5-and-reference.qmd}{viz07})
is the same set, polished.

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  \texttt{visual-audit-note.md} --- at least three issues with category,
  why it matters, and the corrected approach.
\item
  \texttt{corrected-plot.qmd} --- a rerunnable Quarto file that creates
  the corrected plot.
\item
  \texttt{corrected-plot.png} --- the exported corrected plot.
\item
  \texttt{caption.md} --- a caption that states the statistic, data
  source, exclusions, and limitations.
\item
  \texttt{ai-use-note.md} --- if you used AI, explain what it helped
  with and how you checked the result.
\end{itemize}

\section*{Completion Check}\label{completion-check}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Completion Check}

\markright{Completion Check}

You are done with the studio when a peer can rerun your corrected plot,
identify the main comparison without reading your code, and see at least
one explicit limitation in the caption.

issue, write one sentence on what it was, one sentence on why it
matters, and one sentence on how you corrected it.

\texttt{caption.md}}{5. caption.md}}\label{caption.md}

\hyperref[worked-example-descriptive-bmi-visualization]{viz03}:

\begin{quote}
This figure shows {[}measure{]} by {[}group{]} across
{[}time/category{]} using the prepared NHANES Health Equity classroom
dataset. The summary is descriptive and unweighted. Records with missing
{[}variables{]} were excluded. The plot supports comparison of patterns,
not causal claims or population-level estimates.
\end{quote}

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  \textbf{Verify:} the caption names the statistic, the data source, the
  exclusions, and one limitation.
\end{itemize}

\subsection*{\texorpdfstring{7.
\texttt{ai-use-note.md}}{7. ai-use-note.md}}\label{ai-use-note.md}
\addcontentsline{toc}{subsection}{7. \texttt{ai-use-note.md}}

\section*{Accessibility (good practice, not a graded
deliverable)}\label{accessibility-good-practice-not-a-graded-deliverable}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Accessibility (good practice, not a
graded deliverable)}

\markright{Accessibility (good practice, not a graded deliverable)}

A plain-language alt text sentence next to your figure helps
screen-reader users, colour-blind reviewers, and busy policymakers. The
A5 rubric does not score alt text --- it is encouraged practice. If you
want to try it, add \texttt{fig-alt:} as a chunk option:

\section*{ggplot2 quick reference}\label{ggplot2-quick-reference}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{ggplot2 quick reference}

\markright{ggplot2 quick reference}

\begin{longtable}[]{@{}
  >{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{(\linewidth - 4\tabcolsep) * \real{0.3333}}
  >{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{(\linewidth - 4\tabcolsep) * \real{0.3333}}
  >{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{(\linewidth - 4\tabcolsep) * \real{0.3333}}@{}}
\toprule\noalign{}
\begin{minipage}[b]{\linewidth}\raggedright
Need
\end{minipage} & \begin{minipage}[b]{\linewidth}\raggedright
Code
\end{minipage} & \begin{minipage}[b]{\linewidth}\raggedright
Note
\end{minipage} \\
\midrule\noalign{}
\endhead
\bottomrule\noalign{}
\endlastfoot
Load packages & \texttt{library(ggplot2)} & Every session \\
Set data + mapping & \texttt{ggplot(df,\ aes(x,\ y))} & The starting
point \\
Points & \texttt{geom\_point()} & Use \texttt{alpha} for overlap \\
Lines connecting summaries & \texttt{geom\_line()\ +\ geom\_point()} &
Better than \texttt{geom\_smooth()} for descriptive cycle data \\
Bars / columns & \texttt{geom\_col()} & For pre-aggregated counts \\
Labels & \texttt{labs(title,\ subtitle,\ x,\ y,\ caption)} & Caption
goes under the figure \\
Honest y-axis & \texttt{expand\_limits(y\ =\ 0)} & Start at zero unless
you justify otherwise \\
Theme & \texttt{theme\_minimal()} & Clean default \\
Save figure &
\texttt{ggsave("plot.png",\ plot,\ width\ =\ 6,\ height\ =\ 4)} &
Code-generated, not screenshot \\
\end{longtable}

\section*{Visual audit checklist}\label{visual-audit-checklist}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Visual audit checklist}

\markright{Visual audit checklist}

A short version of the five questions from
\hyperref[how-a-plot-can-mislead]{viz04}:

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  Title and caption do not imply causation.
\item
  Y-axis starts at 0 or has a stated reason.
\item
  Geometry suits descriptive data (no smoother across categorical x).
\item
  Weighted vs unweighted is stated.
\item
  Missingness and exclusions are disclosed.
\item
  Small-cell counts are visible or filtered out.
\end{itemize}

\section*{Five quick knowledge
checks}\label{five-quick-knowledge-checks}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Five quick knowledge checks}

\markright{Five quick knowledge checks}

\begin{enumerate}
\def\labelenumi{\arabic{enumi}.}
\tightlist
\item
  What are the five moving parts of a ggplot?
\item
  Name two failure modes from \hyperref[how-a-plot-can-mislead]{viz04}
  and one fix for each.
\item
  Why is \texttt{geom\_smooth(method\ =\ "lm")} rarely appropriate for
  NHANES cycle data?
\item
  What two lines of comment should appear above any AI-drafted chunk?
\item
  What is the difference in scope between Assignment 5 and the M1
  \texttt{initial-visual-plan.md}?
\end{enumerate}

\section*{Where this goes next}\label{where-this-goes-next-6}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Where this goes next}

\markright{Where this goes next}

Next week (\href{../week07-eda-ai-audit/index.qmd}{Week 7}) builds the

\part{Week 7: EDA, Table 1 \& AI Auditing}

\chapter*{Week 7: EDA, Table 1 \& AI
Auditing}\label{week-7-eda-table-1-ai-auditing-1}
\addcontentsline{toc}{chapter}{Week 7: EDA, Table 1 \& AI Auditing}

\markboth{Week 7: EDA, Table 1 \& AI Auditing}{Week 7: EDA, Table 1 \&
AI Auditing}

\section*{Overview}\label{overview-6}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Overview}

\markright{Overview}

This week builds the audit trail behind a descriptive analysis. You will
define an analysis cohort, check missingness, create a Table 1-style
summary, and audit a planted-error starter that mimics common
AI-assisted mistakes.

EDA is treated as \textbf{evidence stewardship}: every exclusion,
statistic, and interpretation should be visible enough for another
person to rerun and challenge.

\section*{Objectives}\label{objectives-1}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Objectives}

\markright{Objectives}

By the end of the week you can:

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  define and document an analysis cohort before summarizing;
\item
  report missingness for key variables rather than dropping rows
  silently;
\item
  create a Table 1-style descriptive summary with N, mean, and SD;
\item
  distinguish what descriptive statistics claim from what they cannot
  claim;
\item
  audit code and prose for correctness, reproducibility, interpretation,
  and stewardship errors.
\end{itemize}

\section*{Connection}\label{connection-6}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Connection}

\markright{Connection}

Week 6 focused on whether visual design makes a descriptive claim honest
and clear. Week 7 documents the cohort, missingness, and summary
statistics behind those claims. Week 8 then turns one audited
descriptive finding into a dashboard-style KT prototype.

\section*{Case Study Data Analysis}\label{case-study-data-analysis-4}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Case Study Data Analysis}

\markright{Case Study Data Analysis}

The \textbf{NHANES Health Equity data spine} now supports code-reading,
EDA, visualization, and reproducibility checks. Use the cached CSV/RDS
for routine class work; a CDC retrieval script exists for advanced
users.

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  Cached RDS:
  \texttt{examples/nhanes-equity/data/nhanes\_equity\_v6.rds}
\item
  CSV snapshot:
  \texttt{examples/nhanes-equity/data/nhanes\_equity\_v6.csv}
\item
  Case-study README: \texttt{examples/nhanes-equity/README.md}
\end{itemize}

The default classroom path is to use the cached data so the analysis
work is reproducible without internet access.

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[numbers=left,,]
\FunctionTok{library}\NormalTok{(readr)}
\FunctionTok{library}\NormalTok{(dplyr)}

\NormalTok{candidate\_paths }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \FunctionTok{c}\NormalTok{(}
  \StringTok{"examples/nhanes{-}equity/data/nhanes\_equity\_v6.csv"}\NormalTok{,}
  \StringTok{"../../examples/nhanes{-}equity/data/nhanes\_equity\_v6.csv"}
\NormalTok{)}

\NormalTok{nhanes\_path }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}}\NormalTok{ candidate\_paths[}\FunctionTok{file.exists}\NormalTok{(candidate\_paths)][}\DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{]}
\ControlFlowTok{if}\NormalTok{ (}\FunctionTok{is.na}\NormalTok{(nhanes\_path)) \{}
  \FunctionTok{stop}\NormalTok{(}\StringTok{"Could not find examples/nhanes{-}equity/data/nhanes\_equity\_v6.csv"}\NormalTok{)}
\NormalTok{\}}

\NormalTok{nhanes\_analysis }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \FunctionTok{read\_csv}\NormalTok{(nhanes\_path, }\AttributeTok{show\_col\_types =} \ConstantTok{FALSE}\NormalTok{)}

\NormalTok{nhanes\_analysis }\SpecialCharTok{|\textgreater{}}
  \FunctionTok{summarise}\NormalTok{(}
    \AttributeTok{rows =} \FunctionTok{n}\NormalTok{(),}
    \AttributeTok{missing\_bmi =} \FunctionTok{sum}\NormalTok{(}\FunctionTok{is.na}\NormalTok{(BMI)),}
    \AttributeTok{missing\_income =} \FunctionTok{sum}\NormalTok{(}\FunctionTok{is.na}\NormalTok{(IncomeGroup))}
\NormalTok{  )}
\CommentTok{\#\textgreater{} \# A tibble: 1 x 3}
\CommentTok{\#\textgreater{}     rows missing\_bmi missing\_income}
\CommentTok{\#\textgreater{}    \textless{}int\textgreater{}       \textless{}int\textgreater{}          \textless{}int\textgreater{}}
\CommentTok{\#\textgreater{} 1 105626        9356           9415}

\NormalTok{nhanes\_analysis }\SpecialCharTok{|\textgreater{}}
  \FunctionTok{filter}\NormalTok{(}\SpecialCharTok{!}\FunctionTok{is.na}\NormalTok{(BMI), }\SpecialCharTok{!}\FunctionTok{is.na}\NormalTok{(IncomeGroup), Age }\SpecialCharTok{\textgreater{}=} \DecValTok{20}\NormalTok{, Age }\SpecialCharTok{\textless{}=} \DecValTok{80}\NormalTok{) }\SpecialCharTok{|\textgreater{}}
  \FunctionTok{group\_by}\NormalTok{(IncomeGroup) }\SpecialCharTok{|\textgreater{}}
  \FunctionTok{summarise}\NormalTok{(}
    \AttributeTok{n =} \FunctionTok{n}\NormalTok{(),}
    \AttributeTok{mean\_bmi =} \FunctionTok{round}\NormalTok{(}\FunctionTok{mean}\NormalTok{(BMI), }\DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{),}
    \AttributeTok{.groups =} \StringTok{"drop"}
\NormalTok{  )}
\CommentTok{\#\textgreater{} \# A tibble: 3 x 3}
\CommentTok{\#\textgreater{}   IncomeGroup            n mean\_bmi}
\CommentTok{\#\textgreater{}   \textless{}chr\textgreater{}              \textless{}int\textgreater{}    \textless{}dbl\textgreater{}}
\CommentTok{\#\textgreater{} 1 High Income (\textgreater{}3.5) 16330     28.6}
\CommentTok{\#\textgreater{} 2 Low Income (\textless{}1.3)  15293     29.4}
\CommentTok{\#\textgreater{} 3 Middle Income      19228     29.3}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\section*{Definition of done}\label{definition-of-done-3}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Definition of done}

\markright{Definition of done}

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  Cohort restrictions are stated before results are interpreted.
\item
  Missingness is checked and reported before complete-case summaries are
  used.
\item
  Table labels match the statistic actually computed.
\item
  The audit identifies at least five planted issues across correctness,
  reproducibility, interpretation, and stewardship.
\item
  M2 preliminary analysis renders from source and explains what the
  descriptive results do not prove.
\end{itemize}

\section*{What students leave with}\label{what-students-leave-with-2}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{What students leave with}

\markright{What students leave with}

A reproducible EDA note and a habit of treating AI-generated summaries
as drafts that must be checked against the data, code, and stewardship
context.

\chapter*{EDA as Evidence
Stewardship}\label{eda-as-evidence-stewardship}
\addcontentsline{toc}{chapter}{EDA as Evidence Stewardship}

\markboth{EDA as Evidence Stewardship}{EDA as Evidence Stewardship}

\section*{Goal}\label{goal-4}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Goal}

\markright{Goal}

Last week you turned a descriptive summary into a clear figure. This
week you build the evidence trail behind that summary. The phrase
``exploratory data analysis'' often sounds casual --- \emph{``just look
at the data''}. In a health-data project it is not casual at all: every
exclusion, every statistic, and every interpretation should be visible
enough that another person can rerun your work and challenge it.

Think of EDA as the \textbf{paperwork} that defends a finding. If the
paperwork is missing, the finding cannot survive an audit.

\section*{The four artifacts this week
produces}\label{the-four-artifacts-this-week-produces}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{The four artifacts this week produces}

\markright{The four artifacts this week produces}

Every page this week supports one of these four:

\begin{enumerate}
\def\labelenumi{\arabic{enumi}.}
\tightlist
\item
  A \textbf{cohort definition} that names exactly who is in the analysis
  sample.
\item
  A \textbf{missingness table} that says how much of each key variable
  is missing.
\item
  A \textbf{Table 1} that summarizes the cohort with N, mean (SD), and
  equivalents.
\item
  A \textbf{methods note} that states what the table can and cannot
  claim.
\end{enumerate}

If any one of the four is absent, the work is not ready for Milestone 2
or Assignment 6.

\section*{Why this matters for AI
work}\label{why-this-matters-for-ai-work}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Why this matters for AI work}

\markright{Why this matters for AI work}

When you ask an AI co-pilot for ``a Table 1,'' the model will produce
one. It will not check whether the cohort makes sense, whether
missingness was reported first, or whether the labels match the
statistics. Those are your responsibilities. The four-artifact workflow
is what makes that responsibility manageable: each artifact is a small
piece you can audit on its own.

The four audit categories you will use this week ---
\textbf{correctness, reproducibility, interpretation, stewardship} ---
are introduced on \href{eda05-ai-audit-categories.qmd}{eda05} and
practiced in the \href{eda06-studio-planted-errors.qmd}{studio}.

\section*{What ``descriptive, unweighted, non-causal''
means}\label{what-descriptive-unweighted-non-causal-means}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{What ``descriptive, unweighted,
non-causal'' means}

\markright{What ``descriptive, unweighted, non-causal'' means}

You will see this phrase repeatedly. Each word matters:

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  \textbf{Descriptive:} the table reports what is in the data, not what
  the population looks like.
\item
  \textbf{Unweighted:} we are not using NHANES survey weights, so the
  numbers describe the prepared classroom dataset, not US adults.
\item
  \textbf{Non-causal:} the table cannot tell you that one variable
  \emph{causes} another. Descriptive comparison is not causal inference.
\end{itemize}

Saying this in your methods note is how you defend the table from being
misread.

\section*{Where AI fits}\label{where-ai-fits-1}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Where AI fits}

\markright{Where AI fits}

Same rule as Week 3 and Week 6: every AI-drafted chunk in your
\texttt{.qmd} gets a two-line
\texttt{\#\ AI\ prompt:\ ...\ \#\ Verified:\ ...} comment. The
verification step on Table 1 work specifically checks: cohort filter,
missingness handling, label/statistic match, and interpretation.

\section*{What you produce this
week}\label{what-you-produce-this-week-1}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{What you produce this week}

\markright{What you produce this week}

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  A rerunnable \texttt{eda-note.qmd} with cohort definition, missingness
  table, and Table 1 (the Assignment 6 deliverable, also the M2
  preliminary analysis).
\item
  A short audit note identifying at least five issues in a planted-error
  starter.
\item
  A revised provenance/stewardship statement suitable for reuse in M2.
\end{itemize}

\section*{Where this goes next}\label{where-this-goes-next-7}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Where this goes next}

\markright{Where this goes next}

\href{eda02-cohort-and-missingness.qmd}{eda02} walks through cohort
definition and missingness reporting --- the first two of the four
