\chapter*{Cohort and Missingness}\label{cohort-and-missingness}
\addcontentsline{toc}{chapter}{Cohort and Missingness}

\markboth{Cohort and Missingness}{Cohort and Missingness}

\section*{Where this fits}\label{where-this-fits-6}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Where this fits}

\markright{Where this fits}

\hyperref[eda-as-evidence-stewardship]{eda01} named the four artifacts
your EDA work produces. This page covers the first two: how to define
and document an analysis cohort, and how to report missingness
\emph{before} you summarize anything. Both habits are the foundation of
every Table 1 you will make for the rest of the term.

\section*{Why cohort definition comes
first}\label{why-cohort-definition-comes-first}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Why cohort definition comes first}

\markright{Why cohort definition comes first}

A dataset is not an analysis sample. The NHANES Health Equity CSV
contains records of all ages, with some variables intentionally coded as
\texttt{NA}. If you summarize without restricting, you are answering a
question the data cannot answer cleanly.

A cohort definition is a one-sentence answer to \emph{``who is in this
analysis?''} --- written down before you compute any statistics. Common
examples:

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  ``Adults age 20-80 in the prepared classroom dataset.''
\item
  ``Female respondents with non-missing BMI.''
\item
  ``Records from NHANES cycles 2013-2014 and 2015-2016.''
\end{itemize}

Each restriction has a reason. The reason goes in the methods note.

\section*{Documenting the cohort in
code}\label{documenting-the-cohort-in-code}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Documenting the cohort in code}

\markright{Documenting the cohort in code}

This pattern shows up in the \href{eda03-worked-example.qmd}{worked
example}. You will reuse it for A6 and M2.

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[numbers=left,,]
\FunctionTok{library}\NormalTok{(readr)}
\FunctionTok{library}\NormalTok{(dplyr)}
\FunctionTok{library}\NormalTok{(knitr)}

\NormalTok{candidate\_paths }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \FunctionTok{c}\NormalTok{(}
  \StringTok{"examples/nhanes{-}equity/data/nhanes\_equity\_v6.csv"}\NormalTok{,}
  \StringTok{"../../examples/nhanes{-}equity/data/nhanes\_equity\_v6.csv"}
\NormalTok{)}
\NormalTok{data\_path }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}}\NormalTok{ candidate\_paths[}\FunctionTok{file.exists}\NormalTok{(candidate\_paths)][}\DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{]}
\NormalTok{nhanes }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \FunctionTok{read\_csv}\NormalTok{(data\_path, }\AttributeTok{show\_col\_types =} \ConstantTok{FALSE}\NormalTok{)}

\NormalTok{analysis\_df }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}}\NormalTok{ nhanes }\SpecialCharTok{|\textgreater{}}
  \FunctionTok{filter}\NormalTok{(Age }\SpecialCharTok{\textgreater{}=} \DecValTok{20}\NormalTok{, Age }\SpecialCharTok{\textless{}=} \DecValTok{80}\NormalTok{)}

\NormalTok{cohort\_counts }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \FunctionTok{tibble}\NormalTok{(}
  \AttributeTok{step =} \FunctionTok{c}\NormalTok{(}\StringTok{"Prepared CSV rows"}\NormalTok{, }\StringTok{"After age 20{-}80 restriction"}\NormalTok{),}
  \AttributeTok{n =} \FunctionTok{c}\NormalTok{(}\FunctionTok{nrow}\NormalTok{(nhanes), }\FunctionTok{nrow}\NormalTok{(analysis\_df))}
\NormalTok{)}

\FunctionTok{kable}\NormalTok{(cohort\_counts)}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

The \texttt{cohort\_counts} table is your \textbf{flow diagram in text
form}. It shows N before and after each restriction. If a peer asks
``how many people are in your analysis?'', this table is the answer.

\begin{tcolorbox}[enhanced jigsaw, colback=white, opacitybacktitle=0.6, breakable, coltitle=black, bottomtitle=1mm, bottomrule=.15mm, left=2mm, opacityback=0, leftrule=.75mm, rightrule=.15mm, toptitle=1mm, colbacktitle=quarto-callout-note-color!10!white, titlerule=0mm, colframe=quarto-callout-note-color-frame, arc=.35mm, title=\textcolor{quarto-callout-note-color}{\faInfo}\hspace{0.5em}{Tester note}, toprule=.15mm]

Capture: a screenshot of the rendered \texttt{cohort\_counts} table in
the Quarto preview. Beginners often skip this step; seeing it labeled
clearly helps them recognize what they are missing.

\end{tcolorbox}

\section*{Why missingness comes before the
summary}\label{why-missingness-comes-before-the-summary}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Why missingness comes before the summary}

\markright{Why missingness comes before the summary}

A complete-case summary silently drops rows. If you compute
\texttt{mean(BMI,\ na.rm\ =\ TRUE)} without first showing how many BMI
values were missing, your reader has no idea how representative the mean
is.

The audit habit is: \textbf{report missingness first, then summarize,
then interpret.} Never the other order.

\section*{Counting missingness}\label{counting-missingness}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Counting missingness}

\markright{Counting missingness}

The cleanest beginner pattern:

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[numbers=left,,]
\NormalTok{key\_vars }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \FunctionTok{c}\NormalTok{(}\StringTok{"BMI"}\NormalTok{, }\StringTok{"Age"}\NormalTok{, }\StringTok{"IncomeGroup"}\NormalTok{, }\StringTok{"Gender"}\NormalTok{, }\StringTok{"Race"}\NormalTok{, }\StringTok{"Cycle"}\NormalTok{)}

\NormalTok{missingness }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \FunctionTok{tibble}\NormalTok{(}\AttributeTok{variable =}\NormalTok{ key\_vars) }\SpecialCharTok{|\textgreater{}}
  \FunctionTok{mutate}\NormalTok{(}
    \AttributeTok{missing\_n =} \FunctionTok{vapply}\NormalTok{(analysis\_df[key\_vars], }\ControlFlowTok{function}\NormalTok{(x) }\FunctionTok{sum}\NormalTok{(}\FunctionTok{is.na}\NormalTok{(x)), }\FunctionTok{numeric}\NormalTok{(}\DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{)),}
    \AttributeTok{missing\_percent =} \FunctionTok{round}\NormalTok{(}\DecValTok{100} \SpecialCharTok{*}\NormalTok{ missing\_n }\SpecialCharTok{/} \FunctionTok{nrow}\NormalTok{(analysis\_df), }\DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{)}
\NormalTok{  )}

\FunctionTok{kable}\NormalTok{(missingness)}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

This produces a small table: one row per key variable, with the missing
count and the percent missing. Three rules apply when you write or
accept code like this:

\begin{enumerate}
\def\labelenumi{\arabic{enumi}.}
\tightlist
\item
  \textbf{Pick \texttt{key\_vars} deliberately.} Only include variables
  you will actually use in the Table 1 or downstream summary.
\item
  \textbf{Use the denominator from the cohort, not the raw dataset.}
  Missingness as a percent of \emph{your cohort} tells the right story.
\item
  \textbf{Report it, even if it is small.} A row that says ``BMI
  missing: 2 (0.1\%)'' is still informative.
\end{enumerate}

\begin{tcolorbox}[enhanced jigsaw, colback=white, opacitybacktitle=0.6, breakable, coltitle=black, bottomtitle=1mm, bottomrule=.15mm, left=2mm, opacityback=0, leftrule=.75mm, rightrule=.15mm, toptitle=1mm, colbacktitle=quarto-callout-note-color!10!white, titlerule=0mm, colframe=quarto-callout-note-color-frame, arc=.35mm, title=\textcolor{quarto-callout-note-color}{\faInfo}\hspace{0.5em}{Tester note}, toprule=.15mm]

Capture: a screenshot of the rendered \texttt{missingness} table for the
adult cohort. Beginners need to see what ``percent missing per
variable'' actually looks like.

\end{tcolorbox}

\section*{What ``complete-case'' means and why it can bias the
summary}\label{what-complete-case-means-and-why-it-can-bias-the-summary}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{What ``complete-case'' means and why it
can bias the summary}

\markright{What ``complete-case'' means and why it can bias the summary}

After you see the missingness table, you have a choice. The simplest
path is \textbf{complete-case analysis}: drop rows where any key
variable is missing. This is what most beginner tutorials show.

That choice is reasonable for a classroom Table 1, but it is not
automatically unbiased. If the rows you drop are systematically
different from the rows you keep (for example, people with missing
income may be different from people with reported income), your summary
tilts in a direction you cannot see from the table alone.

The honest move is to:

\begin{enumerate}
\def\labelenumi{\arabic{enumi}.}
\tightlist
\item
  Report missingness \emph{first}.
\item
  Filter to complete cases \emph{with the missingness disclosed}.
\item
  Add a sentence in the methods note acknowledging the complete-case
  assumption.
\end{enumerate}

You will see this exact sequence in the
\href{eda03-worked-example.qmd}{worked example}.

\section*{A short audit habit}\label{a-short-audit-habit}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{A short audit habit}

\markright{A short audit habit}

After you produce a cohort plus missingness table, ask three questions:

\begin{enumerate}
\def\labelenumi{\arabic{enumi}.}
\tightlist
\item
  Could a reader tell who was excluded and why?
\item
  Is the percent missing reported for every variable in the downstream
  Table 1?
\item
  Did I describe the cohort \emph{before} I described the summary?
\end{enumerate}

If any answer is ``no'', revise before the table.

\section*{Where this goes next}\label{where-this-goes-next-8}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Where this goes next}

\markright{Where this goes next}

\href{eda03-worked-example.qmd}{eda03} is the first end-to-end worked
example: cohort, missingness, Table 1, methods note --- all four
artifacts in one rerunnable Quarto document.

\chapter*{Worked Example: EDA and Table
1}\label{worked-example-eda-and-table-1}
\addcontentsline{toc}{chapter}{Worked Example: EDA and Table 1}

\markboth{Worked Example: EDA and Table 1}{Worked Example: EDA and Table
1}

\section*{Where this fits}\label{where-this-fits-7}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Where this fits}

\markright{Where this fits}

You met the four-artifact workflow on
\hyperref[eda-as-evidence-stewardship]{eda01} and practiced cohort
definition and missingness reporting on
\hyperref[cohort-and-missingness]{eda02}. This page is the first
end-to-end EDA of the term: load the cached CSV, restrict to an adult
cohort, report missingness, produce a Table 1, and write a short methods
note that survives audit. The same workflow underwrites Assignment 6 and
Milestone 2.

\section*{Goal}\label{goal-5}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Goal}

\markright{Goal}

This worked example creates a small Table 1-style descriptive summary
from the NHANES Health Equity CSV snapshot. The goal is transparent EDA:
define the cohort, check missingness, summarize key variables, and write
a methods note that does not overclaim.

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[numbers=left,,]
\FunctionTok{library}\NormalTok{(readr)}
\FunctionTok{library}\NormalTok{(dplyr)}
\FunctionTok{library}\NormalTok{(knitr)}

\NormalTok{candidate\_paths }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \FunctionTok{c}\NormalTok{(}
  \StringTok{"examples/nhanes{-}equity/data/nhanes\_equity\_v6.csv"}\NormalTok{,}
  \StringTok{"../../examples/nhanes{-}equity/data/nhanes\_equity\_v6.csv"}
\NormalTok{)}

\NormalTok{data\_path }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}}\NormalTok{ candidate\_paths[}\FunctionTok{file.exists}\NormalTok{(candidate\_paths)][}\DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{]}
\ControlFlowTok{if}\NormalTok{ (}\FunctionTok{is.na}\NormalTok{(data\_path)) \{}
  \FunctionTok{stop}\NormalTok{(}\StringTok{"Could not find examples/nhanes{-}equity/data/nhanes\_equity\_v6.csv"}\NormalTok{)}
\NormalTok{\}}

\NormalTok{nhanes }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \FunctionTok{read\_csv}\NormalTok{(data\_path, }\AttributeTok{show\_col\_types =} \ConstantTok{FALSE}\NormalTok{)}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\section*{Define the Analysis Cohort}\label{define-the-analysis-cohort}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Define the Analysis Cohort}

\markright{Define the Analysis Cohort}

Decision: restrict to adults age 20-80. This keeps the example focused
on adult BMI summaries and avoids mixing children, adolescents, and the
oldest age-coded records into one classroom table.

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[numbers=left,,]
\NormalTok{analysis\_df }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}}\NormalTok{ nhanes }\SpecialCharTok{|\textgreater{}}
  \FunctionTok{filter}\NormalTok{(Age }\SpecialCharTok{\textgreater{}=} \DecValTok{20}\NormalTok{, Age }\SpecialCharTok{\textless{}=} \DecValTok{80}\NormalTok{)}

\NormalTok{cohort\_counts }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \FunctionTok{tibble}\NormalTok{(}
  \AttributeTok{step =} \FunctionTok{c}\NormalTok{(}\StringTok{"Prepared CSV rows"}\NormalTok{, }\StringTok{"Rows after age 20{-}80 restriction"}\NormalTok{),}
  \AttributeTok{n =} \FunctionTok{c}\NormalTok{(}\FunctionTok{nrow}\NormalTok{(nhanes), }\FunctionTok{nrow}\NormalTok{(analysis\_df))}
\NormalTok{)}

\FunctionTok{kable}\NormalTok{(cohort\_counts)}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\begin{longtable}[]{@{}lr@{}}
\toprule\noalign{}
step & n \\
\midrule\noalign{}
\endhead
\bottomrule\noalign{}
\endlastfoot
Prepared CSV rows & 105626 \\
Rows after age 20-80 restriction & 57137 \\
\end{longtable}

\section*{Missingness Check}\label{missingness-check}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Missingness Check}

\markright{Missingness Check}

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[numbers=left,,]
\NormalTok{key\_vars }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \FunctionTok{c}\NormalTok{(}\StringTok{"BMI"}\NormalTok{, }\StringTok{"Age"}\NormalTok{, }\StringTok{"IncomeGroup"}\NormalTok{, }\StringTok{"Gender"}\NormalTok{, }\StringTok{"Race"}\NormalTok{, }\StringTok{"Cycle"}\NormalTok{)}

\NormalTok{missingness }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \FunctionTok{tibble}\NormalTok{(}\AttributeTok{variable =}\NormalTok{ key\_vars) }\SpecialCharTok{|\textgreater{}}
  \FunctionTok{mutate}\NormalTok{(}
    \AttributeTok{missing\_n =} \FunctionTok{vapply}\NormalTok{(analysis\_df[key\_vars], }\ControlFlowTok{function}\NormalTok{(x) }\FunctionTok{sum}\NormalTok{(}\FunctionTok{is.na}\NormalTok{(x)), }\FunctionTok{numeric}\NormalTok{(}\DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{)),}
    \AttributeTok{missing\_percent =} \FunctionTok{round}\NormalTok{(}\DecValTok{100} \SpecialCharTok{*}\NormalTok{ missing\_n }\SpecialCharTok{/} \FunctionTok{nrow}\NormalTok{(analysis\_df), }\DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{)}
\NormalTok{  )}

\FunctionTok{kable}\NormalTok{(missingness)}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\begin{longtable}[]{@{}lrr@{}}
\toprule\noalign{}
variable & missing\_n & missing\_percent \\
\midrule\noalign{}
\endhead
\bottomrule\noalign{}
\endlastfoot
BMI & 1009 & 1.8 \\
Age & 0 & 0.0 \\
IncomeGroup & 5395 & 9.4 \\
Gender & 0 & 0.0 \\
Race & 0 & 0.0 \\
Cycle & 0 & 0.0 \\
\end{longtable}

\section*{Table 1-Style Summary}\label{table-1-style-summary}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Table 1-Style Summary}

\markright{Table 1-Style Summary}

This example stratifies by \texttt{IncomeGroup} and reports N plus mean
(SD) for BMI and age. It is unweighted and descriptive.

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[numbers=left,,]
\NormalTok{mean\_sd }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \ControlFlowTok{function}\NormalTok{(x) \{}
  \FunctionTok{sprintf}\NormalTok{(}\StringTok{"\%.1f (\%.1f)"}\NormalTok{, }\FunctionTok{mean}\NormalTok{(x, }\AttributeTok{na.rm =} \ConstantTok{TRUE}\NormalTok{), }\FunctionTok{sd}\NormalTok{(x, }\AttributeTok{na.rm =} \ConstantTok{TRUE}\NormalTok{))}
\NormalTok{\}}

\NormalTok{table1 }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}}\NormalTok{ analysis\_df }\SpecialCharTok{|\textgreater{}}
  \FunctionTok{filter}\NormalTok{(}\SpecialCharTok{!}\FunctionTok{is.na}\NormalTok{(IncomeGroup)) }\SpecialCharTok{|\textgreater{}}
  \FunctionTok{group\_by}\NormalTok{(IncomeGroup) }\SpecialCharTok{|\textgreater{}}
  \FunctionTok{summarise}\NormalTok{(}
    \AttributeTok{N =} \FunctionTok{n}\NormalTok{(),}
    \StringTok{\textasciigrave{}}\AttributeTok{BMI, mean (SD)}\StringTok{\textasciigrave{}} \OtherTok{=} \FunctionTok{mean\_sd}\NormalTok{(BMI),}
    \StringTok{\textasciigrave{}}\AttributeTok{Age, mean (SD)}\StringTok{\textasciigrave{}} \OtherTok{=} \FunctionTok{mean\_sd}\NormalTok{(Age),}
    \AttributeTok{.groups =} \StringTok{"drop"}
\NormalTok{  )}

\FunctionTok{kable}\NormalTok{(table1)}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\begin{longtable}[]{@{}lrll@{}}
\toprule\noalign{}
IncomeGroup & N & BMI, mean (SD) & Age, mean (SD) \\
\midrule\noalign{}
\endhead
\bottomrule\noalign{}
\endlastfoot
High Income (\textgreater3.5) & 16520 & 28.6 (6.3) & 49.7 (16.0) \\
Low Income (\textless1.3) & 15646 & 29.4 (7.5) & 47.4 (18.1) \\
Middle Income & 19576 & 29.3 (7.0) & 50.0 (18.4) \\
\end{longtable}

\section*{Methods Note Template}\label{methods-note-template}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Methods Note Template}

\markright{Methods Note Template}

\begin{quote}
We used the prepared NHANES Health Equity classroom CSV snapshot. The
analysis cohort included records with age 20-80. We summarized BMI and
age by income group using unweighted descriptive statistics. Missing
values were checked before summarizing. These summaries describe the
prepared class dataset and should not be interpreted as causal effects
or population estimates.
\end{quote}

\section*{Stewardship Note Template}\label{stewardship-note-template}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Stewardship Note Template}

\markright{Stewardship Note Template}

\begin{quote}
The dataset is a prepared classroom snapshot derived from public-use
NHANES files. It should be used for descriptive learning activities, not
for individual-level inference or small-cell claims. Reports should cite
the source, describe exclusions, and avoid uploading row-level records
to external AI tools.
\end{quote}

\section*{Survey Design Note}\label{survey-design-note}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Survey Design Note}

\markright{Survey Design Note}

NHANES has a complex survey design. This classroom Table 1 is
intentionally unweighted so that the workflow is easy to audit. A
population-representative NHANES analysis would require the appropriate
weights, strata, and primary sampling units.

\section*{Reproducibility Notes}\label{reproducibility-notes}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Reproducibility Notes}

\markright{Reproducibility Notes}

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  The data path is relative to the repository.
\item
  The cohort restriction is stated before the table.
\item
  Missingness is reported before complete-case summaries.
\item
  The table labels distinguish N, mean, and SD.
\item
  The methods note states that the analysis is descriptive and
  non-causal.
\end{itemize}

\section*{Where this goes next}\label{where-this-goes-next-9}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Where this goes next}

\markright{Where this goes next}

\href{eda04-descriptive-statistics-claims.qmd}{eda04} takes a step back
to ask what each statistic in this table actually \emph{claims}.
\href{eda05-ai-audit-categories.qmd}{eda05} introduces the four audit
categories you will use to evaluate a deliberately flawed version of
this analysis in the \href{eda06-studio-planted-errors.qmd}{eda06}
studio.

\chapter*{What Each Statistic Claims (and Does
Not)}\label{what-each-statistic-claims-and-does-not}
\addcontentsline{toc}{chapter}{What Each Statistic Claims (and Does
Not)}

\markboth{What Each Statistic Claims (and Does Not)}{What Each Statistic
Claims (and Does Not)}

\section*{Where this fits}\label{where-this-fits-8}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Where this fits}

\markright{Where this fits}

You produced a Table 1 on the
\hyperref[worked-example-eda-and-table-1]{previous page}. Before you
write an interpretation of it, pause and ask: what does each cell in
that table actually \emph{claim}? This page is a short tour through the
five most common descriptive statistics you will see --- what each one
says, what it leaves out, and the most common ways beginners (and AI
tools) mislabel them.

\section*{The five common statistics}\label{the-five-common-statistics}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{The five common statistics}

\markright{The five common statistics}

\begin{longtable}[]{@{}
  >{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{(\linewidth - 4\tabcolsep) * \real{0.3333}}
  >{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{(\linewidth - 4\tabcolsep) * \real{0.3333}}
  >{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{(\linewidth - 4\tabcolsep) * \real{0.3333}}@{}}
\toprule\noalign{}
\begin{minipage}[b]{\linewidth}\raggedright
Statistic
\end{minipage} & \begin{minipage}[b]{\linewidth}\raggedright
What it answers
\end{minipage} & \begin{minipage}[b]{\linewidth}\raggedright
What it does not answer
\end{minipage} \\
\midrule\noalign{}
\endhead
\bottomrule\noalign{}
\endlastfoot
\textbf{N (count)} & How many records are in this group? & Whether the
group is representative of any population \\
\textbf{Mean} & What is the average value? & Whether the distribution is
symmetric, skewed, or has outliers \\
\textbf{SD (standard deviation)} & How spread out are values around the
mean? & Whether the spread is symmetric or driven by a tail \\
\textbf{Median} & What value falls in the middle? & How extreme the
tails are \\
\textbf{IQR (interquartile range)} & What is the spread of the middle
50\%? & The tails outside the middle 50\% \\
\end{longtable}

A Table 1 typically reports \textbf{N} plus either \textbf{mean (SD)} or
\textbf{median (IQR)} depending on the variable.

\section*{When to prefer mean(SD) vs
median(IQR)}\label{when-to-prefer-meansd-vs-medianiqr}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{When to prefer mean(SD) vs median(IQR)}

\markright{When to prefer mean(SD) vs median(IQR)}

Use \textbf{mean (SD)} when the variable is roughly symmetric and
well-behaved. Age in the adult cohort is a reasonable example.

Use \textbf{median (IQR)} when the variable is skewed or has outliers.
Healthcare costs, hospital length of stay, and household income are
usual suspects. The mean of a skewed variable is pulled by the long
tail; the median is not.

If you are unsure, plot a histogram. If you see a long tail on one side,
prefer median (IQR).

\begin{tcolorbox}[enhanced jigsaw, colback=white, opacitybacktitle=0.6, breakable, coltitle=black, bottomtitle=1mm, bottomrule=.15mm, left=2mm, opacityback=0, leftrule=.75mm, rightrule=.15mm, toptitle=1mm, colbacktitle=quarto-callout-tip-color!10!white, titlerule=0mm, colframe=quarto-callout-tip-color-frame, arc=.35mm, title=\textcolor{quarto-callout-tip-color}{\faLightbulb}\hspace{0.5em}{Tip}, toprule=.15mm]

For this course's NHANES classroom Table 1, BMI and age are commonly
reported as mean (SD). That is a reasonable convention for adult
anthropometric variables. State the choice and the reason in your
methods note.

\end{tcolorbox}

\section*{What ``N'' alone does not tell
you}\label{what-n-alone-does-not-tell-you}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{What ``N'' alone does not tell you}

\markright{What ``N'' alone does not tell you}

A cell that says \texttt{N\ =\ 47} is informative only if the reader
knows:

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  which cohort the 47 came from (cohort definition,
  \hyperref[cohort-and-missingness]{eda02});
\item
  how many of those 47 were excluded for missing values (missingness
  table, also \hyperref[cohort-and-missingness]{eda02});
\item
  what the comparison group sizes are (the rest of the Table 1).
\end{itemize}

If any of those three is invisible, the \texttt{N} is not yet
defensible.

\section*{Three common beginner
mistakes}\label{three-common-beginner-mistakes}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Three common beginner mistakes}

\markright{Three common beginner mistakes}

These are the mistakes that show up most often in AI-drafted Table 1
code. The \href{eda06-studio-planted-errors.qmd}{studio} revisits them
on a deliberately flawed starter.

\subsection*{\texorpdfstring{1. Label says ``mean'' but code computes
\texttt{median()}}{1. Label says ``mean'' but code computes median()}}\label{label-says-mean-but-code-computes-median}
\addcontentsline{toc}{subsection}{1. Label says ``mean'' but code
computes \texttt{median()}}

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[numbers=left,,]
\CommentTok{\# WRONG: label and code disagree}
\FunctionTok{summarise}\NormalTok{(}\StringTok{\textasciigrave{}}\AttributeTok{Mean BMI}\StringTok{\textasciigrave{}} \OtherTok{=} \FunctionTok{median}\NormalTok{(BMI, }\AttributeTok{na.rm =} \ConstantTok{TRUE}\NormalTok{))}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

Either change the label to \texttt{Median\ BMI} or change the function
to \texttt{mean()}. Decide which one you want --- do not let the AI
choose silently.

\subsection*{\texorpdfstring{2. SD reported without
\texttt{na.rm\ =\ TRUE}}{2. SD reported without na.rm = TRUE}}\label{sd-reported-without-na.rm-true}
\addcontentsline{toc}{subsection}{2. SD reported without
\texttt{na.rm\ =\ TRUE}}

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[numbers=left,,]
\CommentTok{\# WRONG: returns NA whenever any BMI value is missing}
\FunctionTok{summarise}\NormalTok{(}\AttributeTok{sd\_bmi =} \FunctionTok{sd}\NormalTok{(BMI))}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

If a single \texttt{NA} is present, \texttt{sd(BMI)} returns
\texttt{NA}. Use \texttt{na.rm\ =\ TRUE} and report the missingness
count separately (see \hyperref[cohort-and-missingness]{eda02}).

\subsection*{3. Percent without a
denominator}\label{percent-without-a-denominator}
\addcontentsline{toc}{subsection}{3. Percent without a denominator}

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[numbers=left,,]
\CommentTok{\# WRONG: a "60\% with low income" cell with no N}
\FunctionTok{summarise}\NormalTok{(}\AttributeTok{percent\_low\_income =} \FunctionTok{mean}\NormalTok{(IncomeGroup }\SpecialCharTok{==} \StringTok{"Low Income (\textless{}1.3)"}\NormalTok{) }\SpecialCharTok{*} \DecValTok{100}\NormalTok{)}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

A percent is only meaningful if the reader can see the denominator.
Always include the N in the same row or in an adjacent cell.

\section*{Small-cell suppression: when not to report a
number}\label{small-cell-suppression-when-not-to-report-a-number}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Small-cell suppression: when not to
report a number}

\markright{Small-cell suppression: when not to report a number}

If a stratified cell has very few records (say, fewer than 10),
publishing the exact statistic can be misleading or, in some health data
settings, a privacy risk. The convention is to either:

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  combine the small group with a neighbor and label it clearly, or
\item
  replace the cell with a ``\textless10'' marker.
\end{itemize}

Your classroom Table 1 with \texttt{n\ \textgreater{}=\ 30} per group
(as in the \hyperref[worked-example-eda-and-table-1]{worked example}) is
already cautious. The principle still applies: do not interpret cells
where N is too small to support the claim.

\section*{The audit habit for descriptive
statistics}\label{the-audit-habit-for-descriptive-statistics}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{The audit habit for descriptive
statistics}

\markright{The audit habit for descriptive statistics}

After you build a Table 1, look at each column heading and each row:

\begin{enumerate}
\def\labelenumi{\arabic{enumi}.}
\tightlist
\item
  Does the column heading describe the statistic \emph{actually}
  computed (mean, median, count, percent)?
\item
  Is the denominator visible for every percent?
\item
  Are missing values either filtered out (with the count reported
  elsewhere) or handled with \texttt{na.rm\ =\ TRUE}?
\item
  Are small cells either combined, marked, or excluded?
\end{enumerate}

If any answer is ``no'', revise the table or its labels before you write
the methods note.

\section*{Where this goes next}\label{where-this-goes-next-10}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Where this goes next}

\markright{Where this goes next}

\href{eda05-ai-audit-categories.qmd}{eda05} introduces the four AI-audit
categories that organize every check you have seen so far this week,
ready for the \href{eda06-studio-planted-errors.qmd}{studio} on the
planted-error starter.

\chapter*{Four Audit Categories for AI-Drafted
EDA}\label{four-audit-categories-for-ai-drafted-eda}
\addcontentsline{toc}{chapter}{Four Audit Categories for AI-Drafted EDA}

\markboth{Four Audit Categories for AI-Drafted EDA}{Four Audit
Categories for AI-Drafted EDA}

\section*{Where this fits}\label{where-this-fits-9}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Where this fits}

\markright{Where this fits}

You have a clean Table 1
(\hyperref[worked-example-eda-and-table-1]{eda03}) and a clear-eyed view
of what each statistic claims
(\hyperref[what-each-statistic-claims-and-does-not]{eda04}). This page
introduces the four categories you will use to organize your audit of
AI-drafted EDA work --- both in this week's
\href{eda06-studio-planted-errors.qmd}{studio} and in every future
descriptive analysis you submit.

\section*{The four categories}\label{the-four-categories}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{The four categories}

\markright{The four categories}

\begin{longtable}[]{@{}
  >{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{(\linewidth - 2\tabcolsep) * \real{0.5000}}
  >{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{(\linewidth - 2\tabcolsep) * \real{0.5000}}@{}}
\toprule\noalign{}
\begin{minipage}[b]{\linewidth}\raggedright
Category
\end{minipage} & \begin{minipage}[b]{\linewidth}\raggedright
The question it asks
\end{minipage} \\
\midrule\noalign{}
\endhead
\bottomrule\noalign{}
\endlastfoot
\textbf{Correctness} & Does the code do what the prose says it does? \\
\textbf{Reproducibility} & Could another person rerun this in a fresh
Codespace and get the same output? \\
\textbf{Interpretation} & Does the narrative overclaim what the
descriptive table can support? \\
\textbf{Stewardship} & Does the work respect data provenance, privacy,
and classroom-use boundaries? \\
\end{longtable}

Each issue you find during an audit should be tagged with one of these
four. A good A6 audit note hits at least three of them.

\section*{Beginner-level examples for
each}\label{beginner-level-examples-for-each}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Beginner-level examples for each}

\markright{Beginner-level examples for each}

\subsection*{Correctness}\label{correctness}
\addcontentsline{toc}{subsection}{Correctness}

\begin{itemize}
\item
  \textbf{Before:} Column labeled ``Mean BMI'' but code uses
  \texttt{median()}.
\item
  \textbf{After:} Either rename the column or change the function. Pick
  one and document it.
\item
  \textbf{Before:} Cohort prose says ``adults'' but the filter uses
  \texttt{Age\ \textgreater{}=\ 0,\ Age\ \textless{}=\ 80}.
\item
  \textbf{After:} Decide what ``adults'' means (typically
  \texttt{Age\ \textgreater{}=\ 20}) and make the filter match the
  prose.
\end{itemize}

\subsection*{Reproducibility}\label{reproducibility-1}
\addcontentsline{toc}{subsection}{Reproducibility}

\begin{itemize}
\item
  \textbf{Before:} A path like
  \texttt{"C:/Users/me/Downloads/nhanes.csv"} in the code chunk.
\item
  \textbf{After:} A relative path like
  \texttt{"examples/nhanes-equity/data/nhanes\_equity\_v6.csv"}, with
  the candidate-path pattern used in the worked example so the chunk
  runs from either the repo root or the week folder.
\item
  \textbf{Before:} Missing \texttt{library(...)} calls. The chunk works
  in your Codespace because you ran \texttt{library(tidyverse)} earlier,
  but a fresh render fails.
\item
  \textbf{After:} Every chunk that uses a package names its
  \texttt{library(...)} calls explicitly.
\end{itemize}

\subsection*{Interpretation}\label{interpretation}
\addcontentsline{toc}{subsection}{Interpretation}

\begin{itemize}
\item
  \textbf{Before:} ``The table proves that income group causes
  differences in BMI.''
\item
  \textbf{After:} ``Mean BMI differed across income groups in this
  prepared classroom dataset. This descriptive comparison cannot
  establish cause.''
\item
  \textbf{Before:} ``Because missing values were removed, the table is
  unbiased and ready for publication.''
\item
  \textbf{After:} ``Complete-case filtering removed \texttt{X} rows. If
  the missingness is related to BMI or income, the complete-case summary
  may be biased.''
\end{itemize}

\subsection*{Stewardship}\label{stewardship-1}
\addcontentsline{toc}{subsection}{Stewardship}

\begin{itemize}
\item
  \textbf{Before:} Submitted notebook pastes a row-level NHANES record
  into an external AI chat to ``ask for a Table 1.''
\item
  \textbf{After:} Aggregate to group counts before any external AI is
  used. Never share row-level health data with external tools.
\item
  \textbf{Before:} The methods note does not name the data source, the
  access method, or the classroom-use context.
\item
  \textbf{After:} The methods note says: prepared NHANES Health Equity
  CSV snapshot, derived from public-use NHANES files, for classroom
  learning only.
\end{itemize}

\section*{What goes in an audit note}\label{what-goes-in-an-audit-note}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{What goes in an audit note}

\markright{What goes in an audit note}

Each issue you flag should have three things:

\begin{enumerate}
\def\labelenumi{\arabic{enumi}.}
\tightlist
\item
  \textbf{What} the problem is (one sentence).
\item
  \textbf{Why it matters} in terms of correctness, reproducibility,
  interpretation, or stewardship.
\item
  \textbf{How} you corrected it (one sentence, or a small code block).
\end{enumerate}

This is the structure that A6 expects, and the studio is your rehearsal.

\section*{A worked audit note line}\label{a-worked-audit-note-line}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{A worked audit note line}

\markright{A worked audit note line}

\begin{quote}
\textbf{Issue 3.} The interpretation says ``income group causes
differences in BMI.'' This is an \textbf{interpretation} problem because
a descriptive summary cannot support a causal claim. Corrected: ``Mean
BMI differed across income groups in the classroom dataset; the
comparison is descriptive and does not establish cause.''
\end{quote}

\section*{Stewardship recap: what goes in the provenance
statement}\label{stewardship-recap-what-goes-in-the-provenance-statement}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Stewardship recap: what goes in the
provenance statement}

\markright{Stewardship recap: what goes in the provenance statement}

The provenance/stewardship statement you produce for A6 (and reuse in
M2) should name:

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  the data source (prepared NHANES Health Equity classroom CSV);
\item
  the source of the source (derived from public-use NHANES files at the
  CDC);
\item
  the access method (cached in the repo, no internet access required for
  class work);
\item
  the classroom-use boundary (descriptive learning only, not population
  inference);
\item
  one privacy or stewardship caution (e.g., no row-level data uploaded
  to external AI tools).
\end{itemize}

Five sentences, written carefully once, reused everywhere.

\section*{The audit habit}\label{the-audit-habit}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{The audit habit}

\markright{The audit habit}

Every time you accept AI-drafted EDA code or prose, run through the four
categories:

\begin{enumerate}
\def\labelenumi{\arabic{enumi}.}
\tightlist
\item
  Does the code match the prose? (correctness)
\item
  Will it rerun in a fresh Codespace? (reproducibility)
\item
  Does any sentence overclaim? (interpretation)
\item
  Is the data and AI use respectful of the source and privacy?
  (stewardship)
\end{enumerate}

\section*{Where this goes next}\label{where-this-goes-next-11}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Where this goes next}

\markright{Where this goes next}

\href{eda06-studio-planted-errors.qmd}{eda06} is the in-class studio
where you apply these four categories to a deliberately flawed Table 1
starter.

Planted-Error Audit}

\section*{Where this fits}\label{where-this-fits-10}

\section*{Required Outputs (studio
draft)}\label{required-outputs-studio-draft-1}

\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Where this fits}

\markright{Where this fits}

You have produced the four artifacts in class. This page is the bridge
from studio drafts to the Assignment 6 submission and the Milestone 2

\href{../../assignments/assignment06-eda-ai-audit/README.md}{A6 README}
exactly --- this page explains what goes into each file and how to
verify it.

\section*{The six A6 deliverables,
explained}\label{the-six-a6-deliverables-explained}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{The six A6 deliverables, explained}

\markright{The six A6 deliverables, explained}

Each file goes in
\texttt{assignments/assignment06-eda-ai-audit/submission/}.

\subsection*{\texorpdfstring{1.
\texttt{eda-note.qmd}}{1. eda-note.qmd}}\label{eda-note.qmd}
\addcontentsline{toc}{subsection}{1. \texttt{eda-note.qmd}}

A rerunnable Quarto file that loads the CSV with a relative path,
defines the analysis cohort, reports missingness, produces the Table 1,
and includes a short methods note. Pattern after the
\hyperref[worked-example-eda-and-table-1]{worked example}.

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  \textbf{Verify:} open it in a fresh Codespace, render top to bottom,
  no errors.
\end{itemize}

\subsection*{\texorpdfstring{2.
\texttt{eda-note.html}}{2. eda-note.html}}\label{eda-note.html}
\addcontentsline{toc}{subsection}{2. \texttt{eda-note.html}}

The rendered output of the \texttt{.qmd} above.

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  \textbf{Verify:} open the HTML in the Codespaces preview and confirm
  the cohort flow, missingness table, and Table 1 all appear.
\end{itemize}

\subsection*{\texorpdfstring{3.
\texttt{table1.csv}}{3. table1.csv}}\label{table1.csv}
\addcontentsline{toc}{subsection}{3. \texttt{table1.csv}}

The Table 1 exported as a CSV. Use
\texttt{write\_csv(table1,\ "table1.csv")} from inside the \texttt{.qmd}
so it is generated by code, not by copy-paste.

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  \textbf{Verify:} the CSV opens, has the same row counts as the
  rendered table, and contains N, BMI mean (SD), and age mean (SD) by
  \texttt{IncomeGroup}.
\end{itemize}

\subsection*{\texorpdfstring{4.
\texttt{audit-note.md}}{4. audit-note.md}}\label{audit-note.md}
\addcontentsline{toc}{subsection}{4. \texttt{audit-note.md}}

A short note listing at least five issues you identified in the
planted-error starter
(\hyperref[in-class-studio-planted-error-audit]{studio page}). Use the
structure from
\hyperref[four-audit-categories-for-ai-drafted-eda]{eda05}:

\begin{verbatim}
Issue 1. [What it is]. This is a [category] problem because [why it matters]. Corrected: [how].
\end{verbatim}

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  \textbf{Verify:} five issues, each tagged with one of the four
  categories (correctness, reproducibility, interpretation,
  stewardship), and each with a corrected approach.
\end{itemize}

\subsection*{\texorpdfstring{5.
\texttt{provenance-stewardship-note.md}}{5. provenance-stewardship-note.md}}\label{provenance-stewardship-note.md}
\addcontentsline{toc}{subsection}{5.
\texttt{provenance-stewardship-note.md}}

A short note suitable for reuse in M2. Use the five-sentence template
from \hyperref[four-audit-categories-for-ai-drafted-eda]{eda05}: data
source, source of source, access method, classroom-use boundary, one
privacy caution.

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  \textbf{Verify:} five named items, each in a complete sentence.
\end{itemize}

\subsection*{\texorpdfstring{6.
\texttt{ai-use-note.md}}{6. ai-use-note.md}}\label{ai-use-note.md-1}
\addcontentsline{toc}{subsection}{6. \texttt{ai-use-note.md}}

If you used AI to draft code or prose, name what it helped with and how
you verified the result. If you did not use AI, write one sentence
saying so.

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  \textbf{Verify:} specific to chunks or prompts, not generic.
\end{itemize}

\section*{Reproducibility checklist for
A6}\label{reproducibility-checklist-for-a6}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Reproducibility checklist for A6}

\markright{Reproducibility checklist for A6}

Before you click submit:

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  The \texttt{.qmd} renders in a fresh Codespace with no manual edits.
\item
  The data path is relative.
\item
  \texttt{table1.csv} is written by code (\texttt{write\_csv(...)}).
\item
  Missingness is reported \emph{before} the Table 1, not after.
\item
  The methods note avoids causal language and names the data as
  descriptive and unweighted.
\item
  All six files are committed and pushed.
\end{itemize}

\section*{A methods-note template you can
reuse}\label{a-methods-note-template-you-can-reuse}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{A methods-note template you can reuse}

\markright{A methods-note template you can reuse}

\begin{quote}
We analyzed the prepared NHANES Health Equity classroom CSV snapshot.
The cohort included records with age 20-80. We summarized N, BMI mean
(SD), and age mean (SD) by \texttt{IncomeGroup} using unweighted
descriptive statistics. Records with missing BMI, age, or income were
excluded after the missingness counts were reported. The summary
describes the prepared classroom dataset only and should not be
interpreted as causal effects or population estimates.
\end{quote}

That is five sentences. Use it as a starting point, edit to match your
team's choices.

\section*{A provenance/stewardship sentence you can
reuse}\label{a-provenancestewardship-sentence-you-can-reuse}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{A provenance/stewardship sentence you can
reuse}

\markright{A provenance/stewardship sentence you can reuse}

\begin{quote}
The dataset is a prepared classroom snapshot derived from public-use
NHANES files at the CDC, cached in the course repository for offline
classroom use. It supports descriptive learning activities only --- not
individual-level inference, small-cell claims, or upload of row-level
records to external AI tools.
\end{quote}

\section*{Five quick knowledge
checks}\label{five-quick-knowledge-checks-1}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Five quick knowledge checks}

\markright{Five quick knowledge checks}

\begin{enumerate}
\def\labelenumi{\arabic{enumi}.}
\tightlist
\item
  What is a cohort, in one sentence?
\item
  Why report missingness \emph{before} dropping rows?
\item
  When is median (IQR) more honest than mean (SD)?
\item
  Name the four AI-audit categories.
\item
  Which A6 file feeds the M2 \texttt{methods-note.md}?
\end{enumerate}

\section*{EDA quick reference}\label{eda-quick-reference}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{EDA quick reference}

\markright{EDA quick reference}

\begin{longtable}[]{@{}
  >{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{(\linewidth - 4\tabcolsep) * \real{0.3333}}
  >{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{(\linewidth - 4\tabcolsep) * \real{0.3333}}
  >{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{(\linewidth - 4\tabcolsep) * \real{0.3333}}@{}}
\toprule\noalign{}
\begin{minipage}[b]{\linewidth}\raggedright
Task
\end{minipage} & \begin{minipage}[b]{\linewidth}\raggedright
Code
\end{minipage} & \begin{minipage}[b]{\linewidth}\raggedright
Note
\end{minipage} \\
\midrule\noalign{}
\endhead
\bottomrule\noalign{}
\endlastfoot
Load CSV &
\texttt{read\_csv("examples/nhanes-equity/data/nhanes\_equity\_v6.csv")}
& Relative path \\
Cohort filter &
\texttt{filter(Age\ \textgreater{}=\ 20,\ Age\ \textless{}=\ 80)} &
Document the reason \\
Cohort counts & \texttt{tibble(step,\ n)} table & Flow diagram in
text \\
Missingness &
\texttt{vapply(df{[}vars{]},\ function(x)\ sum(is.na(x)),\ numeric(1))}
& Use the cohort denominator \\
Mean (SD) &
\texttt{sprintf("\%.1f\ (\%.1f)",\ mean(x,\ na.rm=TRUE),\ sd(x,\ na.rm=TRUE))}
& One readable string \\
Group summary &
\texttt{group\_by(IncomeGroup)\ \%\textgreater{}\%\ summarise(...)} &
One row per group \\
Export & \texttt{write\_csv(table1,\ "table1.csv")} & Generated by
code \\
\end{longtable}

\section*{Overview}\label{overview-7}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Overview}

\markright{Overview}

This is the first app-forward week. You will move from audited EDA
outputs (Week 7) to a small dashboard-style Knowledge Translation
product that helps a defined audience explore one descriptive question.

The \textbf{required core} pathway is the
\href{dash03-starter-dashboard.qmd}{Quarto starter dashboard}: it
renders reliably in every Codespace, makes the analysis visible, and is
the file you will adapt for Assignment 7. The Shiny exemplar discussed
on \href{dash05-testing-privacy-and-shiny-demo.qmd}{dash05} is a
\textbf{demonstration only} --- your instructor will run it in class;
you are not required to install Shiny, run the app, or produce A7
deliverables from it.

\section*{Objectives}\label{objectives-2}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Objectives}

\markright{Objectives}

By the end of the week you can:

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  explain the difference between a static report, a Quarto
  dashboard-style product, and a Shiny app;
\item
  render the Quarto starter dashboard locally and adapt one
  audience-facing element;
\item
  apply the 1-1-1-1-1 rule to translate one Week 7 finding into a
  dashboard prototype;
\item
  distinguish text/UI changes (allowed for A7) from analytic changes
  (not allowed for A7);
\item
  test a dashboard locally and record what was checked;
\item
  apply privacy-safe publishing guidance before sharing any health-data
  product.
\end{itemize}

\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Connection}

\markright{Connection}

Week 7 established an auditable descriptive foundation. Week 8 asks how
a non-technical audience will encounter that evidence in a dashboard.
Week 9 turns one dashboard finding into a concise scientific
communication product.

\chapter*{What a Dashboard Is, and Three
Pathways}\label{what-a-dashboard-is-and-three-pathways}
\addcontentsline{toc}{chapter}{What a Dashboard Is, and Three Pathways}

\markboth{What a Dashboard Is, and Three Pathways}{What a Dashboard Is,
and Three Pathways}

\section*{Goal}\label{goal-7}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Goal}

\markright{Goal}

Last week you produced an audited Table 1. This week turns one
descriptive finding from that table into a small \textbf{audience-facing
artifact} --- a dashboard-style KT product. Before you build, get clear
on what a dashboard is, what it is not, and which pathway you will use
in this course.

\section*{A dashboard is not a
report}\label{a-dashboard-is-not-a-report}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{A dashboard is not a report}

\markright{A dashboard is not a report}

A short way to remember the difference:

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  A \textbf{report} tells a fixed story to a known audience. It loads,
  renders, and stays the same.
\item
  A \textbf{dashboard} lets a defined audience answer one question with
  one or two controls. The audience moves through the artifact and pulls
  out the slice that matters to them.
\item
  A \textbf{data warehouse} is neither. It stores everything for someone
  else to query.
\end{itemize}

You are building the middle one. The audience is real (an analyst, a
project team, a briefing group). The control is small (one dropdown, one
filter, or one editable value). The interpretation and the limitation
stay attached to whatever the audience sees.

A dashboard is \textbf{not} a free pass to be sloppy. It can mislead
just as easily as a single figure can. Everything you learned in Weeks 6
and 7 about scale, captioning, missingness, and stewardship still
applies.

course}\label{three-pathways-three-roles-in-this-course}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Three pathways, three roles in this
course}

\markright{Three pathways, three roles in this course}

Shiny and static HTML/JS are shown so you know they exist; they are not

\section*{Decision rule for your term
project}\label{decision-rule-for-your-term-project}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Decision rule for your term project}

\markright{Decision rule for your term project}

When you choose a dashboard pathway for your team's final portfolio, use
the simplest pathway that serves your audience:

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  If a \texttt{.qmd} page with one editable value answers the audience
  question, that is the right choice.
\item
  If real-time interactivity is essential and your team can run/host a
  server, the Shiny pathway is open --- \textbf{with instructor
  approval}, since it is not a default course pathway.
\item
  If a static, public-facing artifact is required, the Week 11 static
  HTML/JS demo path is the option to explore.
\end{itemize}

There is no rubric bonus for using a more complex pathway. The grading
rubric measures audience fit, audit quality, and reproducibility --- not
technical flash.

\section*{What ``dashboard-style'' means for
Quarto}\label{what-dashboard-style-means-for-quarto}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{What ``dashboard-style'' means for
Quarto}

\markright{What ``dashboard-style'' means for Quarto}

You will see this phrase throughout Week 8. It means a Quarto page that:

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  loads the cached classroom dataset with a relative path,
\item
  declares one \textbf{control value} as an editable R variable near the
  top of the file,
\item
  shows one \textbf{table} and one \textbf{visualization} that update
  when the control changes,
\item
  ends with one \textbf{interpretation} sentence and one
  \textbf{limitation} sentence.
\end{itemize}

That is what the \href{dash03-starter-dashboard.qmd}{starter dashboard}
does, and that is what your A7 adaptation will continue.

\section*{What ``demonstration only'' means for
Shiny}\label{what-demonstration-only-means-for-shiny}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{What ``demonstration only'' means for
Shiny}

\markright{What ``demonstration only'' means for Shiny}

You will see the Shiny exemplar run live in class. You will be invited
to open the URL the instructor shares and click around. You will
\textbf{not} be required to:

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  install or load Shiny in your own Codespace,
\item
  run \texttt{shiny::runApp(...)} yourself,
\item
  modify the Shiny app code,
\item
  produce any A7 deliverable from the Shiny app.
\end{itemize}

If you find Shiny interesting and want to explore it for your term
project, talk with your instructor before committing --- Shiny is not a
default approved A7 pathway and not a default approved final-portfolio
pathway.

\chapter*{From One Finding to a Prototype: The 1-1-1-1-1
Rule}\label{from-one-finding-to-a-prototype-the-1-1-1-1-1-rule}
\addcontentsline{toc}{chapter}{From One Finding to a Prototype: The
1-1-1-1-1 Rule}

\markboth{From One Finding to a Prototype: The 1-1-1-1-1 Rule}{From One
Finding to a Prototype: The 1-1-1-1-1 Rule}

\section*{Where this fits}\label{where-this-fits-12}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Where this fits}

\markright{Where this fits}

You know what a dashboard is and which pathway is required
(\hyperref[what-a-dashboard-is-and-three-pathways]{dash01}). This page
is the translation step: how do you take one Week 7 finding and turn it
into a small dashboard prototype? The answer is a rule with five ones.

\section*{The 1-1-1-1-1 rule}\label{the-1-1-1-1-1-rule}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{The 1-1-1-1-1 rule}

\markright{The 1-1-1-1-1 rule}

For your first dashboard prototype, commit to exactly:

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  \textbf{1 audience} --- name a real person or role (e.g., ``a
  public-health analyst preparing a briefing'').
\item
  \textbf{1 question} --- phrased the way the audience would ask it.
\item
  \textbf{1 control} --- one thing the audience can change (one
  dropdown, one filter, one editable value).
\item
  \textbf{1 visualization} --- one chart that updates when the control
  changes.
\item
  \textbf{1 limitation} --- one sentence about what the artifact does
  \emph{not} support.
\end{itemize}

Beginners try to build a dashboard with three controls, four charts, and
a tab system. That dashboard is harder to make, harder to test, and
easier to break. The 1-1-1-1-1 rule keeps you honest.

\section*{A worked example using your Week 7 Table
1}\label{a-worked-example-using-your-week-7-table-1}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{A worked example using your Week 7 Table
1}

\markright{A worked example using your Week 7 Table 1}

Recall the Table 1 from
\hyperref[worked-example-eda-and-table-1]{eda03}: mean BMI by income
group across NHANES cycles. Here is the 1-1-1-1-1 translation:

\begin{longtable}[]{@{}
  >{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{(\linewidth - 2\tabcolsep) * \real{0.5000}}
  >{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{(\linewidth - 2\tabcolsep) * \real{0.5000}}@{}}
\toprule\noalign{}
\begin{minipage}[b]{\linewidth}\raggedright
Field
\end{minipage} & \begin{minipage}[b]{\linewidth}\raggedright
Filled in
\end{minipage} \\
\midrule\noalign{}
\endhead
\bottomrule\noalign{}
\endlastfoot
Audience & A public-health analyst preparing a short briefing on
descriptive BMI patterns \\
Question & ``Among adults age 20-80, how does mean BMI vary across
NHANES cycles for one income group?'' \\
Control & A single \texttt{selected\_income} value the analyst can
change \\
Visualization & A line plot of mean BMI by cycle for the selected income
group \\
Limitation & The summary is descriptive and unweighted; it does not
support causal or population claims \\
\end{longtable}

That is exactly what the \href{dash03-starter-dashboard.qmd}{starter
dashboard} implements.

\section*{How to choose each field for your own
project}\label{how-to-choose-each-field-for-your-own-project}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{How to choose each field for your own
project}

\markright{How to choose each field for your own project}

\subsection*{Audience}\label{audience}
\addcontentsline{toc}{subsection}{Audience}

Be specific. ``The public'' is too broad. ``A first-year MPH student
doing a course assignment'' is specific. ``A regional health officer
planning a fall briefing'' is specific. The more specific the audience,
the easier every later choice becomes.

\subsection*{Question}\label{question}
\addcontentsline{toc}{subsection}{Question}

Phrase it the way your audience would phrase it. Not ``an exploration of
the multivariate structure of NHANES BMI distributions'' but ``How does
average BMI compare across income groups in this dataset?''. Plain
language sharpens the rest of the design.

\subsection*{Control}\label{control}
\addcontentsline{toc}{subsection}{Control}

One control. If you find yourself wanting two, pick the one your
audience would change first. The second can wait for a later version.

Reasonable Quarto controls for this course:

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  An editable R variable near the top of the \texttt{.qmd} (the starter
  pattern --- change the value, re-render).
\item
  A Quarto parameter declared in the YAML header (\texttt{params:}
  block), updated at render time.
\item
  A \texttt{selectInput}-style control inside a Shiny app --- but only
  if you have approval for the Shiny pathway.
\end{itemize}

\subsection*{Visualization}\label{visualization}
\addcontentsline{toc}{subsection}{Visualization}

One figure. Apply everything from Week 6: honest scale, descriptive
title, weighted/unweighted disclosure, missing-value note, caption
template. The dashboard does not relax those standards --- if anything,
it raises them, because an audience-facing artifact reaches more
readers.

\subsection*{Limitation}\label{limitation}
\addcontentsline{toc}{subsection}{Limitation}

One sentence. Use the templates from
\hyperref[four-audit-categories-for-ai-drafted-eda]{eda05} on what
descriptive comparison cannot establish. Place the sentence where the
audience will actually see it --- at the bottom of the figure caption,
or under the table, or beside the control.

\section*{What to write down before you
build}\label{what-to-write-down-before-you-build}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{What to write down before you build}

\markright{What to write down before you build}

Before you touch any code, write three lines in a note:

\begin{enumerate}
\def\labelenumi{\arabic{enumi}.}
\tightlist
\item
  My audience is: \_\_\_\_\_\_
\item
  My audience would ask: \_\_\_\_\_\_
\item
  The control I will offer is: \_\_\_\_\_\_
\end{enumerate}

If you cannot finish those three lines, the dashboard is not ready to
build yet. Go back to Week 7 and pick a finding you can actually frame.

\section*{A note on scope creep}\label{a-note-on-scope-creep}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{A note on scope creep}

\markright{A note on scope creep}

Once you build your first prototype, you will be tempted to add a second
control, a second figure, an ``advanced settings'' panel, or interactive
tooltips. Resist. Every addition multiplies the surface area for
misleading the audience. The A7 rubric does not reward extra controls;
it rewards a clearly framed audience-facing artifact.

\section*{Where this goes next}\label{where-this-goes-next-15}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Where this goes next}

\markright{Where this goes next}

\href{dash03-starter-dashboard.qmd}{dash03} is the working Quarto
starter dashboard that implements the 1-1-1-1-1 worked example. You will
adapt this file for A7.

\markright{Where this fits}

You named the three pathways on

the 1-1-1-1-1 rule on
\hyperref[from-one-finding-to-a-prototype-the-1-1-1-1-1-rule]{dash02}.

\section*{Goal}\label{goal-8}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Goal}

\markright{Goal}

This starter shows how to turn the Week 7 descriptive summary into a
dashboard-style KT product. It uses one editable control value, one
visualization, one table, one interpretation, and one caution --- the
1-1-1-1-1 rule made concrete.

\section*{Audience Question}\label{audience-question}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Audience Question}

\markright{Audience Question}

Audience: a public-health analyst preparing a short briefing on
descriptive BMI patterns in the classroom NHANES equity dataset.

Question: among adults age 20-80, how does mean BMI vary across NHANES
cycles for one selected income group?

\section*{Setup}\label{setup-1}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Setup}

\markright{Setup}

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[numbers=left,,]
\FunctionTok{library}\NormalTok{(readr)}
\FunctionTok{library}\NormalTok{(dplyr)}
\FunctionTok{library}\NormalTok{(ggplot2)}
\FunctionTok{library}\NormalTok{(knitr)}

\NormalTok{candidate\_paths }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \FunctionTok{c}\NormalTok{(}
  \StringTok{"examples/nhanes{-}equity/data/nhanes\_equity\_v6.csv"}\NormalTok{,}
  \StringTok{"../../examples/nhanes{-}equity/data/nhanes\_equity\_v6.csv"}
\NormalTok{)}

\NormalTok{data\_path }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}}\NormalTok{ candidate\_paths[}\FunctionTok{file.exists}\NormalTok{(candidate\_paths)][}\DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{]}
\ControlFlowTok{if}\NormalTok{ (}\FunctionTok{is.na}\NormalTok{(data\_path)) \{}
  \FunctionTok{stop}\NormalTok{(}\StringTok{"Could not find examples/nhanes{-}equity/data/nhanes\_equity\_v6.csv"}\NormalTok{)}
\NormalTok{\}}

\NormalTok{nhanes }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \FunctionTok{read\_csv}\NormalTok{(data\_path, }\AttributeTok{show\_col\_types =} \ConstantTok{FALSE}\NormalTok{)}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\section*{Control}\label{control-1}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Control}

\markright{Control}

In a Shiny app, this would become a dropdown. In this static starter,
change the value of \texttt{selected\_income} and render again.

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[numbers=left,,]
\NormalTok{available\_income\_groups }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \FunctionTok{sort}\NormalTok{(}\FunctionTok{unique}\NormalTok{(}\FunctionTok{na.omit}\NormalTok{(nhanes}\SpecialCharTok{$}\NormalTok{IncomeGroup)))}
\NormalTok{selected\_income }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \StringTok{"Low Income (\textless{}1.3)"}

\ControlFlowTok{if}\NormalTok{ (}\SpecialCharTok{!}\NormalTok{selected\_income }\SpecialCharTok{\%in\%}\NormalTok{ available\_income\_groups) \{}
\NormalTok{  selected\_income }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}}\NormalTok{ available\_income\_groups[}\DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{]}
\NormalTok{\}}

\NormalTok{selected\_income}
\CommentTok{\#\textgreater{} [1] "Low Income (\textless{}1.3)"}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\section*{Summary}\label{summary-1}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Summary}

\markright{Summary}

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[numbers=left,,]
\NormalTok{dashboard\_summary }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}}\NormalTok{ nhanes }\SpecialCharTok{|\textgreater{}}
  \FunctionTok{filter}\NormalTok{(}
\NormalTok{    Age }\SpecialCharTok{\textgreater{}=} \DecValTok{20}\NormalTok{,}
\NormalTok{    Age }\SpecialCharTok{\textless{}=} \DecValTok{80}\NormalTok{,}
\NormalTok{    IncomeGroup }\SpecialCharTok{==}\NormalTok{ selected\_income,}
    \SpecialCharTok{!}\FunctionTok{is.na}\NormalTok{(BMI),}
    \SpecialCharTok{!}\FunctionTok{is.na}\NormalTok{(Cycle)}
\NormalTok{  ) }\SpecialCharTok{|\textgreater{}}
  \FunctionTok{group\_by}\NormalTok{(Cycle) }\SpecialCharTok{|\textgreater{}}
  \FunctionTok{summarise}\NormalTok{(}
    \AttributeTok{n =} \FunctionTok{n}\NormalTok{(),}
    \AttributeTok{mean\_bmi =} \FunctionTok{round}\NormalTok{(}\FunctionTok{mean}\NormalTok{(BMI), }\DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{),}
    \AttributeTok{.groups =} \StringTok{"drop"}
\NormalTok{  )}

\FunctionTok{kable}\NormalTok{(dashboard\_summary)}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\begin{longtable}[]{@{}lrr@{}}
\toprule\noalign{}
Cycle & n & mean\_bmi \\
\midrule\noalign{}
\endhead
\bottomrule\noalign{}
\endlastfoot

\section*{Visualization}\label{visualization-1}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Visualization}

\markright{Visualization}

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[numbers=left,,]
\FunctionTok{ggplot}\NormalTok{(dashboard\_summary, }\FunctionTok{aes}\NormalTok{(}\AttributeTok{x =}\NormalTok{ Cycle, }\AttributeTok{y =}\NormalTok{ mean\_bmi, }\AttributeTok{group =} \DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{)) }\SpecialCharTok{+}
  \FunctionTok{geom\_line}\NormalTok{(}\AttributeTok{linewidth =} \FloatTok{0.8}\NormalTok{, }\AttributeTok{color =} \StringTok{"\#2f6f73"}\NormalTok{) }\SpecialCharTok{+}
  \FunctionTok{geom\_point}\NormalTok{(}\AttributeTok{size =} \FloatTok{2.4}\NormalTok{, }\AttributeTok{color =} \StringTok{"\#2f6f73"}\NormalTok{) }\SpecialCharTok{+}
  \FunctionTok{labs}\NormalTok{(}
    \AttributeTok{title =} \FunctionTok{paste}\NormalTok{(}\StringTok{"Mean BMI for"}\NormalTok{, selected\_income),}
    \AttributeTok{subtitle =} \StringTok{"Adults age 20{-}80 in the prepared NHANES Health Equity classroom dataset"}\NormalTok{,}
    \AttributeTok{x =} \StringTok{"NHANES cycle"}\NormalTok{,}
    \AttributeTok{y =} \StringTok{"Mean BMI"}\NormalTok{,}
    \AttributeTok{caption =} \StringTok{"Descriptive, unweighted classroom summary. Missing BMI, income group, cycle, and out{-}of{-}cohort ages are excluded."}
\NormalTok{  ) }\SpecialCharTok{+}
  \FunctionTok{theme\_minimal}\NormalTok{(}\AttributeTok{base\_size =} \DecValTok{12}\NormalTok{) }\SpecialCharTok{+}
  \FunctionTok{theme}\NormalTok{(}\AttributeTok{axis.text.x =} \FunctionTok{element\_text}\NormalTok{(}\AttributeTok{angle =} \DecValTok{45}\NormalTok{, }\AttributeTok{hjust =} \DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{))}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\pandocbounded{\includegraphics[keepaspectratio]{weeks/week08-dashboard-kt/dash03-starter-dashboard_files/figure-pdf/dashboard-plot-1.pdf}}

\section*{Interpretation}\label{interpretation-1}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Interpretation}

\markright{Interpretation}

For the selected income group, the table and figure describe the mean
BMI pattern across available NHANES cycles in the prepared classroom
dataset. The dashboard should help the audience ask better questions
about pattern, timing, and context rather than treat the line as a
causal explanation.

\section*{Limitation}\label{limitation-1}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Limitation}

\markright{Limitation}

This starter is unweighted and descriptive. It does not account for
NHANES survey design, does not estimate national prevalence, and should
not be used to make causal claims about income and BMI.

\section*{Testing}\label{testing}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Testing}

\markright{Testing}

Before submitting a dashboard-style product:

\begin{enumerate}
\def\labelenumi{\arabic{enumi}.}
\tightlist
\item
  Render this page from the repository root.
\item
  Change \texttt{selected\_income} to another value from
  \texttt{available\_income\_groups}.
\item
  Render again and check that the table, title, and plot all update.
\item
  Verify that the caption still names the dataset, exclusions, and
  limitation.
\item
  Record any warning or error in your testing log.
\end{enumerate}

\section*{Privacy-Safe Publishing}\label{privacy-safe-publishing}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Privacy-Safe Publishing}

\markright{Privacy-Safe Publishing}

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  Use aggregate summaries, not row-level records.
\item
  Avoid small-cell interpretations when \texttt{n} is low.
\item
  State that outputs are descriptive and unweighted.
\item
  Keep cached classroom data separate from any raw NHANES retrieval
  workflow.
\item
  Do not upload row-level data to external AI tools.
\end{itemize}

\chapter*{Audience-Facing Text: What You Can and Cannot Change for
A7}\label{audience-facing-text-what-you-can-and-cannot-change-for-a7}
\addcontentsline{toc}{chapter}{Audience-Facing Text: What You Can and
Cannot Change for A7}

\markboth{Audience-Facing Text: What You Can and Cannot Change for
A7}{Audience-Facing Text: What You Can and Cannot Change for A7}

\section*{Where this fits}\label{where-this-fits-14}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Where this fits}

\markright{Where this fits}

\hyperref[starter-dashboard]{dash03} is the file you will adapt for
Assignment 7. This page draws the line between \textbf{audience-facing
text changes} (allowed for A7) and \textbf{analytic changes} (not
allowed for A7). Knowing where that line is keeps the assignment focused
and keeps your audit trail clean.

\section*{The rule, in one sentence}\label{the-rule-in-one-sentence}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{The rule, in one sentence}

\markright{The rule, in one sentence}

For Assignment 7, you may change anything the audience reads. You may
not change anything the analysis computes.

\section*{Allowed changes (text-only)}\label{allowed-changes-text-only}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Allowed changes (text-only)}

\markright{Allowed changes (text-only)}

These are the kinds of changes the A7 rubric rewards. They sharpen the
audience experience without touching the analysis.

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  \textbf{Rename a label.} ``Cycle'' becomes ``Survey wave (NHANES
  cycle)'' so a non-expert audience knows what to read.
\item
  \textbf{Expand a caption.} A two-word caption becomes a one-sentence
  caption that names the statistic, source, exclusions, and limitation.
\item
  \textbf{Add a help text line.} Beside the control, a sentence that
  says ``This dashboard summarizes adults age 20-80 from the prepared
  classroom dataset.''
\item
  \textbf{Add a limitation sentence.} Beside the figure, one sentence on
  what the artifact does not support.
\item
  \textbf{Improve a README sentence.} A README that says ``Dashboard for
  NHANES'' becomes ``Descriptive dashboard of unweighted mean BMI by
  income group across NHANES cycles, for classroom use.''
\item
  \textbf{Adjust a title for the audience.} A neutral title becomes one
  that names the audience-facing question.
\end{itemize}

\section*{Not allowed for A7 (analytic-only
changes)}\label{not-allowed-for-a7-analytic-only-changes}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Not allowed for A7 (analytic-only
changes)}

\markright{Not allowed for A7 (analytic-only changes)}

These changes touch the analysis. They are not part of A7. If you find a
real analytic issue while testing, raise it with your instructor --- do
not silently change it.

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  \textbf{Changing the cohort filter.} Switching from
  \texttt{Age\ \textgreater{}=\ 20} to \texttt{Age\ \textgreater{}=\ 18}
  is an analytic decision.
\item
  \textbf{Adding or removing a survey weight.} Switching from unweighted
  to weighted is an analytic decision.
\item
  \textbf{Adding a regression line.} Smoothing across cycles is an
  analytic decision (and usually inappropriate --- see
  \hyperref[how-a-plot-can-mislead]{viz04}).
\item
  \textbf{Replacing \texttt{mean()} with \texttt{median()}.} Changing
  the central tendency statistic is an analytic decision.
\item
  \textbf{Changing the grouping variable.} Switching from
  \texttt{IncomeGroup} to \texttt{Education} is an analytic decision.
\end{itemize}

If you are unsure whether a change is text or analytic, ask: ``would a
reader looking at the rendered HTML notice this change if I didn't tell
them?'' If yes, it is probably text. If the \emph{numbers} change, it is
analytic.

\section*{Before / after examples}\label{before-after-examples}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Before / after examples}

\markright{Before / after examples}

\subsection*{Before / after: a label}\label{before-after-a-label}
\addcontentsline{toc}{subsection}{Before / after: a label}

\textbf{Before:}
\texttt{selectInput("strat\_var1",\ "Primary\ Grouping\ (Rows):",\ ...)}

\textbf{After:}
\texttt{selectInput("strat\_var1",\ "Compare\ across\ (rows\ of\ the\ chart):",\ ...)}

The label is friendlier to a non-technical audience. The variable,
choices, and computation are unchanged.

\subsection*{Before / after: a caption}\label{before-after-a-caption}
\addcontentsline{toc}{subsection}{Before / after: a caption}

\textbf{Before:} \texttt{caption\ =\ "BMI\ by\ income"}

\textbf{After:}
\texttt{caption\ =\ "Mean\ BMI\ by\ income\ group\ across\ five\ NHANES\ cycles.\ Adults\ age\ 20-80\ in\ the\ prepared\ classroom\ dataset.\ Unweighted\ descriptive\ summary;\ records\ with\ missing\ BMI,\ income,\ or\ cycle\ are\ excluded."}

The caption now names the statistic, source, cohort, exclusions, and
disclosure. The figure itself is unchanged.

\subsection*{Before / after: a help text
line}\label{before-after-a-help-text-line}
\addcontentsline{toc}{subsection}{Before / after: a help text line}

\textbf{Before:} (no help text near the control)

\textbf{After:} Right under the control:
\texttt{\textgreater{}\ This\ dashboard\ uses\ adults\ age\ 20-80.\ To\ see\ a\ different\ income\ group,\ change\ the\ value\ and\ re-render.}

The audience now understands what the artifact covers and how to use it.

\subsection*{Before / after: a README
sentence}\label{before-after-a-readme-sentence}
\addcontentsline{toc}{subsection}{Before / after: a README sentence}

\textbf{Before:} \texttt{\#\ Dashboard}

\textbf{After:} `\# NHANES Health Equity Classroom Dashboard

Descriptive, unweighted mean BMI by income group across NHANES cycles.
For classroom learning; not for population inference.`

The README now names the audience and the boundary.

\section*{\texorpdfstring{What to record in your A7
\texttt{before-after-text.md}}{What to record in your A7 before-after-text.md}}\label{what-to-record-in-your-a7-before-after-text.md}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{What to record in your A7
\texttt{before-after-text.md}}

\markright{What to record in your A7 \texttt{before-after-text.md}}

A7 asks you to submit one before-after text change. For that file,
paste:

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  the \textbf{original text} verbatim,
\item
  the \textbf{revised text} verbatim,
\item
  one sentence on \textbf{why} the change helps the audience,
\item
  one sentence confirming \textbf{no analytic logic was changed}.
\end{itemize}

That last sentence is what keeps the audit trail clean.

\section*{Part 1 --- Testing the starter
dashboard}\label{part-1-testing-the-starter-dashboard}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Part 1 --- Testing the starter dashboard}

\markright{Part 1 --- Testing the starter dashboard}

The A7 deliverable list asks for a \texttt{testing-log.md}. Use the
routine below for the entry.

\subsection*{A six-step test routine}\label{a-six-step-test-routine}
\addcontentsline{toc}{subsection}{A six-step test routine}

\begin{enumerate}
\def\labelenumi{\arabic{enumi}.}
\tightlist
\item
  \textbf{Render from the repository root.} Open
  \texttt{dash03-starter-dashboard.qmd} in your Codespace and click
  Render. Confirm there are no errors.
\item
  \textbf{Change the control.} Edit \texttt{selected\_income} to a
  different value from \texttt{available\_income\_groups}. Save the
  file.
\item
  \textbf{Render again.} Confirm the title, table, and plot all updated.
\item
  \textbf{Verify the caption.} The caption should still name the
  dataset, exclusions, and limitation.
\item
  \textbf{Open the HTML preview.} Use the Codespaces preview pane and
  confirm the rendered page looks correct in the browser, not just in
  the editor.
\item
  \textbf{Record the test.} In \texttt{testing-log.md}, note the date,
  the command or render path used, the values you tried, and the result.
\end{enumerate}

\begin{tcolorbox}[enhanced jigsaw, colback=white, opacitybacktitle=0.6, breakable, coltitle=black, bottomtitle=1mm, bottomrule=.15mm, left=2mm, opacityback=0, leftrule=.75mm, rightrule=.15mm, toptitle=1mm, colbacktitle=quarto-callout-note-color!10!white, titlerule=0mm, colframe=quarto-callout-note-color-frame, arc=.35mm, title=\textcolor{quarto-callout-note-color}{\faInfo}\hspace{0.5em}{Tester note}, toprule=.15mm]

Capture: a screenshot of the Codespaces Quarto preview showing the
rendered starter dashboard for \emph{two} different
\texttt{selected\_income} values, side by side or in sequence. This is
the most common visual confirmation that the control ``works.''

\end{tcolorbox}

\subsection*{Common failure modes and quick
fixes}\label{common-failure-modes-and-quick-fixes}
\addcontentsline{toc}{subsection}{Common failure modes and quick fixes}

\begin{longtable}[]{@{}
  >{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{(\linewidth - 4\tabcolsep) * \real{0.3333}}
  >{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{(\linewidth - 4\tabcolsep) * \real{0.3333}}
  >{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{(\linewidth - 4\tabcolsep) * \real{0.3333}}@{}}
\toprule\noalign{}
\begin{minipage}[b]{\linewidth}\raggedright
Failure
\end{minipage} & \begin{minipage}[b]{\linewidth}\raggedright
What you see
\end{minipage} & \begin{minipage}[b]{\linewidth}\raggedright
Quick fix
\end{minipage} \\
\midrule\noalign{}
\endhead
\bottomrule\noalign{}
\endlastfoot
Wrong working directory & ``Could not find
examples/nhanes-equity/data/nhanes\_equity\_v6.csv'' & Open the Quarto
file via the Explorer and confirm Codespaces is rooted at the repo
root \\
Missing package & ``there is no package called `ggplot2'\,'' & Run
\texttt{library(ggplot2)} once; if it still fails, raise with your TA
--- do not install at random \\
Stale \texttt{\_freeze} cache & Output reflects the old control value &
Delete the relevant \texttt{\_freeze/} subfolder and re-render; or
change the chunk slightly \\
Preview pane blank & Render succeeds but preview is empty & Click the
preview pane refresh icon, or open the HTML file from \texttt{docs/} (if
configured) \\
\end{longtable}

\begin{tcolorbox}[enhanced jigsaw, colback=white, opacitybacktitle=0.6, breakable, coltitle=black, bottomtitle=1mm, bottomrule=.15mm, left=2mm, opacityback=0, leftrule=.75mm, rightrule=.15mm, toptitle=1mm, colbacktitle=quarto-callout-note-color!10!white, titlerule=0mm, colframe=quarto-callout-note-color-frame, arc=.35mm, title=\textcolor{quarto-callout-note-color}{\faInfo}\hspace{0.5em}{Tester note}, toprule=.15mm]

Capture: one screenshot of each failure state above, with the error
visible. New students stall on these and screenshots in the Survival
Guide help unblock faster.

\end{tcolorbox}

\section*{Part 2 --- Privacy-safe
publishing}\label{part-2-privacy-safe-publishing}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Part 2 --- Privacy-safe publishing}

\markright{Part 2 --- Privacy-safe publishing}

Five rules apply every time you share a dashboard rendering, a
screenshot, or a Quarto preview.

\begin{enumerate}
\def\labelenumi{\arabic{enumi}.}
\tightlist
\item
  \textbf{No row-level data.} Your figures and tables show aggregate
  summaries. Never paste a row-level NHANES record into a screenshot, a
  chat with an external AI, or a public README.
\item
  \textbf{No small-cell interpretations.} If a group has very few
  records, do not interpret the cell. Combine, mark, or exclude the
  group (see \hyperref[what-each-statistic-claims-and-does-not]{eda04}).
\item
  \textbf{State descriptive limits.} Every interpretation should say the
  artifact is descriptive and unweighted. No causal language. No
  ``national prevalence'' language.
\item
  \textbf{Keep cached data separate from raw retrieval.} The course uses
  a cached CSV intentionally. If you write code that retrieves raw
  NHANES from the CDC, document that as separate work --- do not put a
  live API call in your dashboard.
\item
  \textbf{Do not upload row-level data to external AI tools.} Aggregate
  first. The AI conversation can see counts and means; it should not see
  individual records.
\end{enumerate}

\subsection*{\texorpdfstring{What to write in
\texttt{privacy-check.md}}{What to write in privacy-check.md}}\label{what-to-write-in-privacy-check.md}
\addcontentsline{toc}{subsection}{What to write in
\texttt{privacy-check.md}}

A6 and A7 both expect a short privacy check. For A7, write three
sentences:

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  One naming the data source and the classroom-use boundary (reuse from
  \hyperref[four-audit-categories-for-ai-drafted-eda]{eda05}).
\item
  One confirming the dashboard renders aggregate summaries only.
\item
  One confirming you did not share row-level records with any external
  AI tool.
\end{itemize}

\section*{Part 3 --- The Shiny exemplar (demonstration
only)}\label{part-3-the-shiny-exemplar-demonstration-only}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Part 3 --- The Shiny exemplar
(demonstration only)}

\markright{Part 3 --- The Shiny exemplar (demonstration only)}

Your instructor will demo a Shiny app version of the NHANES dashboard in
class. \textbf{You are not required to run this app, install Shiny, or
produce anything from it.} Your A7 deliverable is built from the Quarto
starter dashboard, not from this app.

What the demo will show you:

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  How a Shiny app turns the same kind of analysis into a live,
  server-backed dashboard with reactive controls.
\item
  The trade-offs: more interactivity, but it requires a server (or a
  Codespaces port forward), more packages, and more failure modes.
\item
  Where the app code lives (\texttt{examples/nhanes-equity/app/app.R})
  if you want to read it.
\end{itemize}

If, after watching the demo, you decide you want to use Shiny for your
term-project final dashboard, that pathway is \textbf{not approved by
default}. Talk with your instructor before committing. The Quarto
pathway is the supported default for every student.

\begin{tcolorbox}[enhanced jigsaw, colback=white, opacitybacktitle=0.6, breakable, coltitle=black, bottomtitle=1mm, bottomrule=.15mm, left=2mm, opacityback=0, leftrule=.75mm, rightrule=.15mm, toptitle=1mm, colbacktitle=quarto-callout-warning-color!10!white, titlerule=0mm, colframe=quarto-callout-warning-color-frame, arc=.35mm, title=\textcolor{quarto-callout-warning-color}{\faExclamationTriangle}\hspace{0.5em}{Warning}, toprule=.15mm]

Do \textbf{not} put \texttt{shiny::runApp(...)} in your A7 submission as
your ``dashboard.'' A7 expects an adaptation of the Quarto starter. The
Shiny app is shown to you; it is not what you build on.

\end{tcolorbox}

Dashboard Adaptation}

\section*{Where this fits}\label{where-this-fits-16}

You have everything you need: the worked Quarto starter
(\hyperref[starter-dashboard]{dash03}), the rule for allowed changes
(\hyperref[audience-facing-text-what-you-can-and-cannot-change-for-a7]{dash04}),

\section*{Before you start}\label{before-you-start-2}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Before you start}

\markright{Before you start}

Take 2 minutes to confirm your Codespace is ready:

\begin{enumerate}
\def\labelenumi{\arabic{enumi}.}
\tightlist
\item

  \texttt{examples/nhanes-equity/data/nhanes\_equity\_v6.csv}.
\item
